### NOTICE.

I offer here to enthusiasts of Historical Research those findings which I have made regarding a period in the **History of the Knights Templar** <note>The Knights Templar were a powerful Catholic military order founded in the 12th century; their sudden downfall and the subsequent trials in the 14th century have long been a subject of historical debate and mystery.</note>, which until now has remained buried in a deep obscurity; not because we have lacked the materials necessary to clarify it, but because the prejudices, or the laziness of historians, have prevented them from making a proper use of them. This is a new proof of the labor that history still requires, and how much its form would change if the documents it provides us were examined with care and brought fully into the light.

I flatter myself that I have clarified this matter, so obscure until now, in a manner suited to making the truth appear distinctly. I know that one could push these investigations even further, and I believe that the way in which I have treated this point of history would be capable of yielding several very interesting results. I leave this task to <term>Scholars</term> <note>original: "Savans." Referring to the "Republic of Letters," the international community of intellectuals and academics during the 17th and 18th centuries.</note> who will have more knowledge and leisure, and as much love for the truth as I.

<sig>* 2</sig>

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces their new research into a forgotten era of the Knights Templar, criticizing previous historians for their neglect of primary sources. The author presents their work as a clarifying contribution to the field while inviting more specialized scholars to build upon these findings.</summary>

<keywords>Knights Templar, historical research, historiography, primary sources, scholarly laziness, academic truth</keywords>

<sig>* 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ESSAY</meta>

### NOTICE.

learned member of the Academy of Sciences <note>Likely referring to the Royal Academy of Sciences and Belles-Lettres in Berlin, an institution central to the Enlightenment, of which Nicolai was a prominent member.</note> to which I dedicate this *Essay*, will perhaps take pleasure in continuing the examination of this matter; one has all the more reason to hope for this, as this Academy has particularly dedicated itself to history, and we already have fine monuments <note>In this context, "monuments" refers to the enduring records, published memoirs, and scholarly achievements of the Academy.</note> of its labors.

The subject matter that I treat at the end of this work has only a very distant relationship with my principal object; I nevertheless wished to take advantage of this opportunity to make known—so that they are not lost—certain discoveries that chance led me to make some time ago.

Berlin, this 4th of
March 1782.

->FRÉDÉRIC NICOLAI<-

He also mentions the inclusion of secondary, chance discoveries regarding history that he felt were too important to leave unpublished.</summary>

# ESSAY
## ON THE
### ACCUSATIONS BROUGHT
## AGAINST THE
# TEMPLARS.

---

### FIRST SECTION.

->*Introduction.*<-

THE HISTORY of the Order of the Temple and its sudden destruction is generally known. Several authors have spoken of it; Dupuy <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and librarian to the King, who wrote an influential account of the trial of the Templars based on original documents.</note> and Gürtler <note>Nicolaus Gürtler (1654–1711), author of "Historia Templariorum," a comprehensive history of the order published in the late 17th century.</note> have made it the subject of specific works, and recently Dr. Anton of Görlitz <note>Karl Gottlob Anton (1752–1818), a German jurist and historian who published a study on the Templars in 1781, just before Nicolai's work.</note> has applied himself with tireless activity to drawing it from its original sources; so that it would be useless to enter here into details concerning the history of this Order.

We also know the terrible accusations with which it was charged, accusations upon the truth or falsehood of which the

<summary>This page serves as the formal opening to Frédéric Nicolai’s essay, acknowledging that while the history of the Knights Templar is well-documented by previous scholars, the focus of this work will be the specific, "terrible" accusations that led to their downfall.</summary>

<keywords>Knights Templar, Pierre Dupuy, Nicolaus Gürtler, Karl Gottlob Anton, historiography, accusations, 18th-century scholarship</keywords>

### ESSAYS ON THE ACCUSATIONS

historians do not agree. The greatest number and the best, such as Thomasius, Meusel, and Anton <note>Christian Thomasius, Johann Georg Meusel, and Karl Gottlob Anton were prominent 18th-century Enlightenment scholars who favored a critical, evidence-based approach to history.</note>, justify the Templars.

They attribute their condemnation solely to the greed and hatred of the King of France, Philip the Fair <note>Philip IV (1268–1314), whose debt to the Templars and desire for their wealth are often cited as motives for his attack on the Order.</note>, and to the base compliance of Pope Clement V, his creature <note>Clement V was the first of the Avignon popes; "creature" here implies he was a political puppet installed and controlled by the French King.</note>. On the other hand, some French authors, such as Natalis Alexandre, du Puy, and Daniel <note>Noël Alexandre, Pierre Dupuy, and Gabriel Daniel were historians who often wrote from a Gallican or pro-monarchical perspective, defending the actions of the French crown.</note>, justify or defend the King of France and condemn the Templars, while Gürtler and the author of a work that appeared recently (1) seek, in a manner that is not very consistent, to maintain a middle ground, by acknowledging that the Templars were less guilty than was believed, but also by refusing to allow either the King of France or the Pope to incur the blame for this iniquitous judgment.

It is at least certain that Philip's hatred, to which greed was perhaps joined, was the primary cause of the Templars' downfall. The Order could not have been abolished without the consent of the

---

(1) *History of the Abolition of the Order of the Templars.* Paris 1779. octavo <note>Original: "Hiſtoire de l'abolition de l'Ordre des Templiers. Paris 1779. 8º." This likely refers to the work by Claude Mansuet Jeune.</note>.

### BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.
->A 2<-

King <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...without the consent of the King."</note>. But one cannot deny that the Templars gave cause for their misfortunes through their bad conduct. As Knights and Monastics <term>Religieux: members of a religious order who have taken vows, such as poverty, chastity, and obedience</term>, they were doubly proud. Priestly pride was common to them, it is true, as it was with all the ecclesiastics of their time; but the pride of the military Orders was unbearable, to the ecclesiastics and to secular people alike, because the Knights, though bound by religious vows, were nevertheless not consecrated priests, but merely laymen. As warriors, they felt the superiority that valor and triumphs always provide, and they made others feel this dominance to the point of full satiety.

It was not only recently that the Templars had become disliked, and King Philip was not the only one to hate them. All contemporary historians, when speaking of them, mention their **usurpations**, their passion for extending their **privileges** beyond the limits of their original rule <note>The "Latin Rule" was the code of conduct for the Templars, originally emphasizing humility and poverty, which critics argued the Order had abandoned as they grew wealthy.</note>, and the resentment that their pretensions caused in many people. They sought, as soon as they were able, to withdraw themselves from the authority of the

<summary>This page argues that the Templars' own arrogance and expansion of privileges contributed to their downfall, noting that King Philip IV was not alone in his resentment of the Order. It highlights the friction between the Templars, who were lay warriors with religious vows, and both the established clergy and the general public.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, King Philip the Fair, military orders, ecclesiastical pride, religious vows, social status, Rule of the Templars</keywords>

### ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS
A

Patriarch of Jerusalem (1) and went as far as refusing him the **Tithe** <term>Dîme: A mandatory religious tax, usually one-tenth of one's income or produce, paid to support the church and its clergy.</term>. Now, it is well known what persecutions were faced during the Middle Ages by all those who had the audacity to refuse both obedience and the tithe to the Church.

As early as the year 1199, the Bishop of Tiberias put them under the ban <note>Being "put under the ban" was a severe legal and religious punishment; it typically meant the individuals were excommunicated and stripped of their legal protections, effectively becoming outlaws in the eyes of the Church.</note>, because they were withholding 1300 **bezants** (2) and other assets from him. We find that in 1208, Pope Innocent III—who had done them so much good, and who had removed them from all other jurisdictions so they would depend on Rome alone—that this Pope, I say, complains

---

(1) "Having neglected humility, they withdrew themselves from the Lord Patriarch of Jerusalem, from whom they had received both the institution of their Order and their first benefits, denying him the obedience that their predecessors had shown him; furthermore, by withholding tithes and first-fruits from the churches of God, and by unduly disturbing their possessions, they became a great nuisance." <note>original Latin: "Neglecta humilitate, Domino Patriarchæ Hierosolymitano... facti sunt valde molesti."</note> This is what the Archbishop of Tyre says in his *History of Deeds Done Beyond the Sea, Book XII, Chapter VII*, found in the collection *Deeds of God Through the Franks, page 820*. Matthew Paris says the same thing in his *Greater History, page 56* of the Watts edition, London 1686, large folio. A papal bull from Pope Innocent III had declared them exempt from any other supremacy than that of the Pope. Du Puy, *History of the Templars*, Brussels 1751, in the *Supporting Documents, page 104*.

(2) Currency of the time. <note>A gold or silver coin originally from the Byzantine Empire, widely used in international trade and the Crusader states.</note>

## BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLIERS.
A 3

bitterly of their disorders and their lack of discipline: "vices," he adds, "for which they would have already deserved to lose the apostolic liberties, of which they make such a crying abuse" (1).

It was not only the ecclesiastics who complained of them, but they had also justly angered several secular Princes. In 1200, Leo, King of Armenia, accused them of having refused to fight in his favor against the Infidels, and even of failing to protect his States while he himself was on campaign, despite the fact that they held properties there to the value of twenty thousand **bezants** <term>Bezant: A gold coin originally from Byzantium (Constantinople) that served as the standard international currency in the Mediterranean during the Crusades.</term>.

In the year 1229 they broke faith with Emperor Frederick II (2). This Prince complains in a letter, written in 1244, of their pride and their softness <note>The original French "mollesse" suggests a life of luxury or lack of martial rigor, contrasting with their intended monastic poverty.</note>, and accuses them of secretly conspiring with the Sultan of Kerak (3).

---

> (1) "And although through these and other wicked deeds—which we refrain from elaborating upon more fully here, lest we be forced to take more severe vengeance—they deserve to be stripped of the Apostolic privileges which they so enormously abuse; for he deserves to lose a privilege who abuses the power entrusted to him." <note>original Latin: "Et licet per hæc et alia nefanda... qui commiffa fibi abutitur poteftate."</note> Du Puy, p. 137 & 142.
>
> (2) Matthew Paris, p. 302.

### ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS
A
<meta>catchword: A</meta>

In 1223, they encroached without scruple upon the jurisdiction of the King of England, Henry III, at La Rochelle; and Pope Honorius III, who had so often taken their side against secular Princes, was obliged to reproach them for it (1). This same King of England, outraged by the insolence of these religious men, and especially indignant toward the Knights of Saint John and of the Temple, speaking to the Prior of Saint John, told him to his face that he had firmly resolved to humble these two Orders (2).

*The royal treaty being little valued, so that within the enclosures of the houses of the Temple the aforementioned Sultans and their followers were received with pompous alacrity, and the Templars permitted their superstitions with the invocation of Mahomet and secular luxuries.* <note>original Latin: "regio fœdere parvipenso ut infra claustra domorum templi prædictos Soldanos & suos cum alacritate pomposa receptos, superstitiones suas cum invocatione Machometi, & luxus seculares Templarii paterentur."</note> Matthew Paris, p. 947. See also the Confession of an English Knight, Brother Thomas de Tocci of Thoroldeby, in Du Puy, p. 398.

(1) Du Puy, *Justifying Documents*, p. 147.
(2) *“You Prelates and Religious, but especially the Templars and Hospitallers, have so many liberties and charters that your superfluous possessions make you proud, and your pride makes you mad. Therefore, those things which were imprudently granted must be prudently revoked, and those things which were distributed without consideration must be recalled through consultation. — Does not the Lord Pope sometimes, indeed many times, revoke his own deed? Does he not cancel previously granted charters by adding this obstacle, 'notwithstanding' <term>Notwithstanding: from the Latin 'non obstante,' a legal clause used by the Pope or monarchs to override previous decrees or laws.</term>? Thus I too shall break this and other charters which my predecessors and I have rashly granted.”* <note>original Latin: "Vos Prælati & Religiosi, maxime tamen Templarii & Hospitalarii, tot habetis libertates & chartas... & ego temere concessimus."</note> Matthew Paris, p. 737.

<summary>This page details the growing friction between King Henry III of England and the Templars, including accusations that the Order hosted Muslim leaders with "pompous alacrity" and the King's threat to revoke their legal privileges.</summary>

# [ACCUSATIONS] BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS
<p align="right">A 4</p>

If it is true that Kings had for such a long time held such strong prejudices against this Order, it appears that it would have needed great prudence to maintain itself; but the Templars continued, through their greed, their pride, and their disorderly lives, to make themselves generally detested. When in 1290 the loss of all their possessions in Palestine had forced them to flee to the island of Cyprus, King Henry II received them there, it is true, but fearing their power, he refused them permanent settlements and wished to subject them to a kind of <term>capitation</term> <note>A "capitation" is a poll tax or head tax levied on each individual.</note>: they, in revenge, fomented a revolt that nearly dethroned him (1). They did the same in France a few years later. The Grand Master, although born a vassal of the King, placed at the head of his titles: "By the Grace of God" <note>This formula was traditionally reserved for sovereign monarchs, implying they held their power from God alone, not from a terrestrial lord.</note>, and treated his Sovereign as an equal. It is known that Philip the Fair <note>Philippe IV, known as "le Bel," King of France from 1285 to 1314.</note> constantly defended the rights of the throne against the unprecedented claims of the Church, principally against the pride and harshness of Pope Boniface VIII, and that he was the first who discussed in a manner

Neither of these two authors has indicated the source from which he drew this information, and I know of none for this period.

<center># BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS. #</center>
<p align="right">A 5</p>

disobeyed the decrees of the church as soon as they became advantageous to it <note>The author suggests the Templars were selective in their obedience, prioritizing their own interests over both royal and ecclesiastical law.</note>. (A conduct that, to mention in passing, we have seen the <term>Jesuits</term> <note>A Catholic religious order often accused of political intrigue, which was suppressed in several European countries in the 18th century, shortly before this text was likely written.</note> adopt in our own day.) He suspected them further, and not without basis, of having taken part in the revolt of the Parisians in 1304. It is thus easy to understand that this Prince thought of humbling these troublesome subjects, or even that, being resolved to rid himself of them, he had solicited and obtained the Pope’s consent in advance.

While agreeing with all that precedes—and one must agree, since I rely on the uniform and incontestable testimony of all the writers of the time—does it necessarily follow that all the accusations upon which <term>Philip</term> <note>Philip IV, known as Philip the Fair (reigned 1285–1314).</note> and after him <term>Clement</term> <note>Pope Clement V (reigned 1305–1314), the first Pope of the Avignon Papacy.</note> interrogated a multitude of Knights, were absolutely fabricated; that vengeance and greed alone provided them to this Prince; and that the confessions of the guilty were extracted only through torture? I confess that after a mature examination of the accusations and the legal documents that have reached us, I have not been able to persuade myself of this. In our day, the Courts of the <term>House of Bourbon</term> <note>The ruling dynasty of France and Spain; this likely refers to the mid-18th-century legal actions against the Jesuits.</note> had

### ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS ###

<meta>catchword: les</meta>

...had resolved upon the extinction of the <term>Society of Jesus</term> <note>The Jesuits; a Catholic religious order that faced widespread political opposition in the 18th century, leading to its suppression in several countries and eventually by the Pope in 1773.</note>, long before the Court of Rome had yet been able to resolve itself to it; will it be said, because of that, that its enemies invented the subtleties of Father <term>Busenbaum</term> <note>Hermann Busenbaum (1600–1668), a Jesuit theologian whose writings were controversial for allegedly suggesting that regicide (killing a king) could be justified under certain circumstances.</note> on <term>regicide</term>, and the scandalous writings of the <term>Casuist</term> <note>A person who studies moral problems of conscience; the term was often used pejoratively to describe those using clever but misleading reasoning to justify immoral acts.</note> Sanchez on fornication and adultery?

<term>Philip the Fair</term> <note>King Philip IV of France (reigned 1285–1314), who orchestrated the arrest of the Templars.</note>, to whom the <term>Templars</term> <note>A wealthy and powerful military order of the Crusades that had become a political rival to the French crown.</note> were a burden, and who sought only the opportunity to repress them, like all other religious orders, must have congratulated himself, no doubt, that a happy chance had discovered to him that heretical principles were adopted by a part of the Order and that the accusation was found justified by the confessions of a crowd of Knights. This itself provided him with the means to destroy a body that he initially only wanted to weaken. From then on, the <term>Pope</term> <note>Clement V (reigned 1305–1314), the first of the Avignon Popes, who was under heavy influence from the French King.</note> was no longer in a position to defend it, and Philip made use of the violence with which his century employed the sword and the flame against everything that smacked of <term>heresy</term> <note>Beliefs or practices considered to be at odds with the established teachings of the Church.</note>, to fix all at once and without return a revolution, which a short time before he hardly flattered himself to owe one day, and even imperfectly, to all the mechanisms of his politics.

<summary>The author argues that while King Philip the Fair had political motives for wanting to weaken the Templars, the discovery of heretical scandals—similar to the controversies surrounding the Jesuits in later centuries—provided him the necessary leverage to destroy the Order entirely using the brutal methods of the 14th century.</summary>

What would not have been the satisfaction of the **House of Bourbon** <note>The royal family that ruled France, Spain, and other territories; here referring to their role in the 18th-century suppression of the Jesuits.</note>, if after having resolved, for very different reasons, the extinction of the **Society** <note>The Society of Jesus, or Jesuits.</note>, a happy chance had provided it the means to bring such an accusation and that of obtaining the same confessions? And truly, in **England** and in **Ireland**, as in **France**, a great number of the imprisoned **Templars** admitted they were guilty of their own accord and without being put to the <term>question</term> <note>The "question" refers to judicial torture, a standard legal practice at the time to extract confessions.</note>, and this not in vague terms, but in great detail, affirming what they knew, and persisting in denying what they did not know; this appears clearly from the **interrogations** of the prisoners, of which fragments remain to us, to which one cannot help but add faith, unless one refuses historical authenticity to all judicial acts of that era.

But it is very astonishing that all writers have glided so lightly over the crimes of which the **Templars** were accused. They all equally content themselves with naming them, and then they cry out, that they are horrible! almost incredible! then, according to whether they feel inclined for or against the **Order**, they conclude,

or else, that their frightful principles deserved death or exile; or else, that by force of being horrible and incredible, these charges descend into ridicule; that they must therefore be regarded as calumnies <note>False and malicious statements intended to damage a reputation; slanders.</note> forged by fury and rapacity, and that consequently the **Templars** <note>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, a wealthy and powerful Catholic military order suppressed in 1312.</note> were the innocent victims of the most cruel passions.

No one, from what I have been able to see, has taken the trouble to weigh the accusations themselves, any more than the testimonies of the criminals. No one has thought to apply themselves to each accusation in particular, to seek to distinguish within them what might be true from what the fanaticism of those times must have added in exaggeration. No one has worked to clarify the points on which both the judges and the prisoners may have been mistaken. The former were fanatics in good faith; the others were warriors, of whom perhaps none had ever made <term>orthodoxy</term> <note>Adherence to correct or accepted creeds, especially in religion.</note> or <term>heresy</term> <note>Belief or opinion contrary to orthodox religious doctrine.</note> the object of his reflections and who, consequently, did not know too well themselves to which category the practices and customs of their **Order** belonged. misunderstandings such a state of mind must have given rise to. It would have been quite easy, however, to pay attention to these different matters, since the majority of the materials for the history of the **Templars**—and particularly all that remains to us of the confessions <note>These were the admissions of guilt, often obtained under torture or the threat of it, during the trials of 1307–1314.</note> of the condemned Knights—have been gathered in the new edition of the work by **du Puy** <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar and royal librarian who compiled a famous but biased collection of documents regarding the trial of the Templars.</note>, which is otherwise of little interest. These precious documents were well worth the effort of being examined to extract the truth from them; yet, I do not see that anyone has attempted to do so. 

I could, on the contrary, prove beyond any doubt that judgments have been passed based on du Puy’s narrative without the writers having seen the <term>supporting documents</term> <note>Original: "pieces justificatives." These are the primary source materials, such as trial transcripts and official decrees, used to verify historical claims.</note>, which often directly refute his text. Nor has anyone, to my knowledge, undertaken to compare the conclusions and judgments of modern authors with the original records. These judgments, following an abuse all too common in this branch of history, are passed from one work to another without any critical examination. Such an investigation would reveal whether both the accusations and the defenses have been viewed from their proper perspective.

As much as has been possible for me, I have used only contemporary authors, and especially the actual confessions of the accused **Knights**: I shall endeavor to put forward nothing that does not rest upon such authorities; and when the obscurity of history compels me to risk making conjectures, I will not only present them as such, but I also agree that they should only be granted a degree of probability proportional to their similarity to the best-known facts. My sole objective in these investigations has been to discover the truth, without regard for the judgment with which the **Inquisitors** <note>The ecclesiastical officers in charge of the trial, who were often criticized for using torture to extract confessions.</note> followed the confessions of the accused; for even supposing that my work were to make the **Templars** appear more criminal than has been believed until now, I am nonetheless far from condemning them, or justifying the inhumanity of their persecutors. BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS. 15

furthermore, when we trace back to the source of certain erroneous notions, we often find they have a nobler cause than those of the orthodox systems presented to us as truth. I do not wish to resemble those historians of heresy who, with Irenaeus or Epiphanius <note>Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD) and Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD) were early Christian bishops and "heresiologists" known for their exhaustive, often polemical catalogs of groups they considered heretical.</note>, place all error on the side of the heretics and all truth on their own. Is there, then, a single human principle that contains one or the other without mixture? But it is more honest to search for the truth among the false than to do the opposite; and unfortunately, this latter process has been that of nearly all historians of human opinions, among whom I include the authors of the so-called *Ecclesiastical History*.

1st. A great number of the accused Templars absolutely denied all the charges brought against their Order (1).

It is true that if one is satisfied with a superficial glance at the surviving interrogations undergone by the Templars, it may seem strange that despite the confessions of several members, a greater number persisted in denying everything. For my part, I find it even stranger that among the multitude of writers who have occupied themselves with the history of this Order and this famous trial, there is not a single one who has paid attention to a circumstance that seems to me of the greatest importance: that the Templars had two, if not three, degrees of <term>Reception</term> <note>The formal ceremony of initiation or admission into the Order, which the author suggests involved different levels of secrets.</note>. This appears clearly through voluntary confessions made in different places. I will content myself with giving a few examples here.

It is likely that this took place during a General Chapter <note>The *Capitulum Generale* was the supreme assembly of the Order, where high-level decisions were made and solemn rituals performed.</note>, for the third class of the Order's members was the one admitted to General Chapters; there, an image was displayed to the Knights, which we shall examine in another place. Several of those who confess to a second reception, however, claim to have no knowledge of this figure; others, on the contrary, positively assert having seen it in several General Chapters (1). Others say with the same assurance that they never saw it, because they had never been in a General Chapter (2). We see, then, that independent of the members who had been admitted twice, there was yet a third class, secret and select, whose members were finally fully incorporated into the Order, such that this third degree was to the Templars what the fourth vow is to the Jesuits (3) <note>In the Jesuit Order (Society of Jesus), the "fourth vow" of special obedience to the Pope is taken only by the most senior and trusted members, granting them full inclusion in the Society’s inner governance.</note>.

Those who were admitted to the General Chapter <note>The supreme assembly of the Order's leadership.</note> took part in the government of the Order and, consequently, in all its secrets; and I find it quite probable that among the motives for adopting a specific figure, with a particular name in the general assemblies, they sought a way to facilitate recognition between the Knights. In this way, by this common sign, one who had important secrets to communicate could see at once to what extent another brother—whom he was perhaps meeting for the first time—was informed of the Order’s designs. For, supposing that the latter was not in a state to describe the simulacrum <note>original: "simulacre"; a physical image or representation, often used in the trial records to refer to the alleged idol of the Templars.</note>, and that he did not know the name of <term>Baphemetus</term> <note>A Latinized form of Baphomet, the name of the mysterious image or head the Templars were accused of worshipping.</note>, it was a sign that he had never been to a General Chapter, and that things which were to remain secret had to be hidden from him; it is not difficult to conceive that Knights who spent—

I turn now to the objection based on the fact that several Templars denied the crimes of which the Order was accused. But this objection loses its force if one considers what so many different testimonies place beyond doubt: that the Order, independently of the common reception <note>The standard initiation ceremony for the majority of members.</note>, recognized one, if not two, other forms of reception. Consequently, it is no longer surprising if the Knights of the first profession <note>The general membership who took the standard public vows.</note> were ignorant of and denied the things confessed by those who had received a particular and secret rule. The denials of the former thus do not in any way invalidate the confessions of the latter; and even considering that their number must have been very small, there is reason to view the quantity and uniformity of their confessions as a new proof of their veracity.

Based on this general overview of what occurred during this momentous era, it is easy to see that the King of France, Philip the Fair <note>King Philip IV (r. 1285–1314), known as "le Bel" for his looks, who orchestrated the downfall of the Templars.</note>, could not have extracted through the torments of the rack <note>original: "gehenne," a term referring to hellish torture.</note>, nor bought through his bribes, all the confessions of the accused. One also sees that the secret statutes of the Order were most widespread in France and, after France, in England. These two nations had taken the primary part in the Crusades. The great treasury and the archives of the Templars were in Paris (1). Since the end of the twelfth century, all the Grand Masters had been French, and before that time, several leaders of the Order had been of the same nation, which still claims its founder, Hugh of Payens (2). Having therefore

---

<margin>The year 1216.</margin> But the Grand Master <term>Grand Master: The supreme authority of the Order of the Temple.</term> who reigned in 1216 is everywhere named Thomas or Peter of Montaigu, and never Berauld; however, du Cange <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a famous scholar of the Middle Ages.</note> was probably led into error by this passage from du Puy <note>Pierre du Puy, the historian whose 17th-century account of the trial is being critiqued here.</note>, which he perhaps did not examine with enough care, when he places the era of this custom under Grand Master Berard or Beraud in 1273: in which he has been followed by all subsequent writers. If it is true, as the confession of the English proves, that this practice passed from France to England between 1250 and 1260, it is impossible that it was introduced in France only after 1270. I am inclined to suspect du Cange and du Puy of having mistaken Peraut for Berauld, and the English establishment for the French establishment. In matters of history, one cannot be too exact.

# ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS
C 2
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

> II°. *Confessions were extracted through the Question, and several Templars issued a retraction of them, in which they persisted until their death.*

Here again, we see the humane sentiments of the great Thomasius <note>Christian Thomasius (1655–1728) was a celebrated German jurist and philosopher of the Enlightenment who famously campaigned against the use of judicial torture and witch trials.</note>, and one cannot help but approve of them wholeheartedly. The <term>Question: A technical term for judicial torture used during interrogation to extract a confession.</term> is, without doubt, a very unreliable means of discovering the truth; but if confessions due to torment count for little in favor of an accusation, then disavowals made after the Question do no more to disprove it. There are examples where the Question forced the confession of very real crimes, while it failed to extract confessions that were just as well-founded and of which the criminals kept the secret until death. It therefore proves nothing, either for or against, unless it is bolstered by the presence of many other means of evidence.

When one maturely weighs all the circumstances of this famous trial, one sees that neither the use of the Question nor the retractions of the accused prove the falsity of the accusations.

One cannot say which ones were tortured in France, or

# BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.

everything there is marked by weakness, inconsistency, and fear.

In 1308, the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay</note> appeared before three Cardinals. Having responded in a manner entirely opposite to their expectations, they themselves betrayed the malice of their hearts by falling back on this foolish pretext <note>original Latin: *fatuo hoc prætextu*</note>. This is all the more notable because they interrogated him three days later, where "he showed no stupidity, but the greatest prudence" <note>original Latin: *nullam stultitiam, sed summam prudentiam exhibuit*</note>. But the facts are quite different, and one cannot conclude anything against the judges from this. Four days before the confrontation with the Grand Master, a man in secular clothing presented himself to the Commissioners and told them: that his name was Johannes de Molayo <note>John of Molay; the author is distinguishing him from the Grand Master, Jacques (James) de Molay</note> and, showing them his seal, he added:

> "That he had been in the Order for ten years, without
> "having seen anything criminal there; that he was
> "nevertheless ready to do and to sign everything they
> "wished; that he would be very curious to learn
> "the fate of the Order; that as for his person,
> "the Commissioners could dispose of him, &
> "that he begged them to provide him with
> "necessities, given that he was poor."

It is of this man, whose every word smelled of madness, that the Commissioners said:
> "from the appearance and consideration of his person,
> "actions, gestures, and speech, that he was very simple
> "or foolish, and not of sound mind." <note>original Latin: *ex aspectu & consideratione personæ suæ, actuum, gestium & loquelæ, quod erat valde simplex vel fatuus, & non bene compos mentis.*</note>

They sent him to the Bishop of Paris, so that the latter might provide him with the same interview given to the other Templars who had left the Order. They were quite right to do so, and this action is a proof of their impartiality; for if they had wanted false witnesses, this man could have served their purposes.

### ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS

Guillaume de Plafiano, who happened to be there by chance, took him aside and warned him to be on his guard so as not to cover himself with shame and lose himself without necessity. It is quite clear that this stranger reminded him of his previous depositions <note>In a legal context, a deposition is a witness's sworn out-of-court testimony.</note>: without this consideration, could a simple defense of the Order, undertaken by its head, have ever turned to his shame, even assuming it had not appeared sufficient to the judges? Consequently, the Grand Master began to waver from that point on. He confessed "that unless the greatest precautions were taken, he risked ruining his cause. For this reason, he begged the Commissioners to postpone the matter until the following Friday, so that he might have time to reflect upon it." This delay was granted, and they even agreed in advance to extend it if necessary.

### BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.

> "He said that he was only a poor, unlettered knight (1). Having learned from one of the briefs that had been read to him that the Pope had reserved the right to interrogate him and the high officials of the Order personally, he would not enter into any detail for the moment; but that he was ready to appear before the Holy Father as soon as he was ordered to do so, and then he would strive to present what might tend toward the glory of God and the Church."

This was nothing more than a simple evasion. The bull *Doing Mercy* <note>original: "Faciens misericordiam." A papal bull issued by Clement V in August 1308 which organized the investigation into the Templars.</note>, which is at issue here, is dated the 2nd of the Ides of August, that is to say, August 12, 1308 (2). # ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS

Guillaume de *Plafiano* <note>William of Plaisians (d. 1313), a prominent lawyer and influential advisor to King Philip the Fair of France.</note>, who happened to be there by chance, took him aside and warned him to be on his guard so as not to cover himself with disgrace and ruin himself unnecessarily. (*he should take care lest he dishonor himself or perish without cause* <note>original: "habeat providere ne se vituperaret vel perderet sine causa"</note>). It is clear that this stranger reminded him of his previous depositions <note>Sworn testimonies. The author suggests that Plaisians was subtly threatening de Molay by reminding him of his earlier confessions, which would make any sudden defense of the Order look like perjury.</note>: without this consideration, could a simple defense of the Order, undertaken by its leader, ever have turned to his shame, even assuming it had not appeared sufficient to the judges? Thus, the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note> began to waver from that point on. He admitted “that unless he took the greatest precautions, he risked ruining his cause. For this reason, he begged the Commissioners to postpone the matter until the following Friday, so that he might have time to reflect on it.” This delay was granted, and they even agreed in advance to extend it if necessary.

# ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS

The Pope says there, in truth, that his intention had initially been to personally interrogate the Grand Master and some of the other Knights; but he added also that their illnesses having prevented them from making the journey, he had deputed three Cardinals to examine them. (1) It is before these Commissioners—

---

—the year 1307. It is stated there that the Cardinals were to arrive at their destination within three weeks; which leads one to presume that the interrogation took place at the beginning of 1308. Baluzius <term>Étienne Baluze (1630–1718), a French scholar known for his collection of papal records</term>, p. 122, places under the heading of the year 1308 the report that the Cardinals made to the King. According to this document, which is undated, the interrogation was held on the *Saturday after the Assumption of Mary* <note>original Latin: "die fabbati poft affumptionem Mariæ"</note>. This feast is usually celebrated on August 15, and yet mention of it is made in this bull <term>A formal document or edict issued by the Pope</term>, which is dated August 12, 1308. Leibnitz <term>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a polymath and historian</term>, in his *Supplement to the Code of the Law of Nations* <note>original title: "Mantiffa Cod. Jur. Gent."</note>, p. 76, attributes this same bull to the year 1307; but he is mistaken, for it is dated to the third year of the pontificate <note>Clement V began his reign in late 1305, making his third year 1307–1308</note>. Pilgram, in his *Chronological Calendar of the Middle Ages* <note>original title: "Calendarium Chronol. med. ævi"</note>, p. 205, claims that until the time of Charlemagne <term>The 8th-century King of the Franks who standardized many religious observances</term> the Assumption of the Virgin was celebrated in France on January 18, and that even in the twelfth century this feast had not yet been transferred throughout the kingdom to August 15. If it were established that in the 14th century it was still celebrated in France on January 15, the difficulty <note>Referring to the logical contradiction of a bull dated August 12 mentioning a feast on August 15 that had already passed</note> would be resolved.

(1) The terms of this bull and of the two interrogations suffice to refute what du Puy <term>Pierre du Puy (1582–1651), a royal librarian who wrote a history of the Templar trial</term> reports, p. 62, namely, that after the first...

### BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.

...that the Grand Master had made at Chinon, in 1308, the deposition <note>A formal statement or testimony given under oath</note> that he was now revoking. Those who composed the new tribunal had also been appointed by the Pope for the preparation of the trial. Why then did he appeal to the Pope? Questioned by the Commissioners as to whether he had any objection to make against the legality of their inquiry in Paris, [it is said that] the Grand Master was brought before the Pope, first at Lyon and then at Poitiers, and that he confirmed his deposition there under penalty of death, both he and several other Knights. The author of the *History of the Abolition of the Order of the Templars* and Mr. Anton <note>Karl Gottlob von Anton (1752–1818), a German historian who wrote extensively on the Templars</note> also recount this fact very differently. They say that the interview at Poitiers preceded the detention of the Templars; that the Grand Master and the Knights, informed of the accusations being raised against them, had rejected them as so many slanders, while asking the Pope to investigate them thoroughly. There is still some inaccuracy in this account; it was the King, and not the Grand Master, who saw the Pope at Lyon and at Poitiers, as it appears from the Brief <term>A formal letter or decree issued by the Pope, typically less solemn than a "bull"</term> cited by Baluzius <term>Étienne Baluze (1630–1718), a French scholar and expert on medieval canon law</term> in the *Lives of the Avignon Popes* <note>original Latin: "in Vitis Paparum Avenoniens."</note>, Vol. II, p. 76. When one writes history, one should not allow oneself the slightest assertion without having consulted the original sources. It is because they neglected this precaution that all the historians of the Order of the Templars have allowed a multitude of mistakes to escape them—mistakes which I have discovered by returning to the sources, but which I cannot correct here.

<summary>The author critiques previous historians for inaccurately claiming the Grand Master met the Pope at Poitiers before his arrest, clarifying that it was actually King Philip the Fair who met the Pope.</summary>

# ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS

...inquiries?" he replied, "No, and that they had only to continue (1)."

He did not, therefore, recuse <note>To challenge or disqualify a judge on the grounds of prejudice or conflict of interest</note> his judges; and even supposing he had suspected their impartiality, this reproach would have also fallen upon the Pope, who had appointed them. Furthermore, what could he have said to the Holy Father that he could not have said just as well to his deputies? The Grand Master’s <term>The supreme head of the Order of the Knights Templar</term> action was thus merely a result of his reflections; he had had time to convince himself, through his previous depositions <note>Formal testimonies given under oath, in this case referring to his earlier statements at Chinon and Paris</note>, that he could never honorably take upon himself the defense of the Order, and thus no other course of action remained for him but to appeal to the Pope.

In the meantime, and although the Grand Master did not wish to take charge of the defense of the Order before the Commissioners <note>The papal commission of bishops and scholars appointed by Clement V to investigate the Templars in Paris</note>, he nevertheless declared to them the three following points for *the discharge of his conscience* (*for the discharge of his conscience* <note>original Latin: "ad exonerationem conscientiae suae"</note>): "I,

...of a man who seeks excuses, and whether a simple retraction made after the fact could destroy two previous confessions, perfectly voluntary and consistent; to which were added, moreover, the testimonies of a number of other Knights, and especially those of several foreign Knights. <page-num>51</page-num>
# BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.
<sig>C 2</sig>

challenge them and demand a confrontation. This is how a man of honor, unjustly accused, would have acted had he been sure of his case. But the Grand Master <note>Jacques de Molay (c. 1243–1314), the final Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note> maintains an absolute silence regarding all these circumstances, and his silence sufficiently proves what we know today through other testimonies: namely, that he was guilty.

His disavowal following the publication of the sentence can no longer invalidate the facts for which history has demonstrated the evidence. Furthermore, most modern authors recount quite inaccurately his final confrontation with three other Superiors <note>These were the high-ranking officers of the Order: Guy d'Auvergne, Hugues de Pairaud, and Geoffroi de Gonneville.</note> of the Order. For instance, the author of the *History of the Abolition of the Order of the Templars* <note>Likely a reference to the work of Pierre Dupuy or a similar historian who compiled records of the trial, often criticized by later authors for their interpretation of the events.</note> reports it in a flowery style; and to cast it in a marvelous light, he implies that before the publication of the sentence, the Grand Master and the Dauphin of Auvergne <note>Guy d'Auvergne, the Preceptor of Normandy.</note>, *seized by a holy emotion*, had retracted their depositions as if by a *sudden inspiration*. He asserts that they had been promised liberty, provided they withdrew this retraction. He places a long and beautiful speech into the mouth of the Grand Master. There <note>original catchword: "Voilà"</note>

This account is simple and true; it presents the facts quite differently, if I am not mistaken, than the first narrative, whose affected ornaments only serve to obscure the truth.

We readily pity the fate of this unfortunate man <note>Jacques de Molay, the last Grand Master of the Knights Templar.</note> and his brothers; but we shall be no less severe in our historical inquiries. It is very probable that the Grand Master, seeing that he could no longer regain his freedom, preferred death to perpetual imprisonment, and that he perhaps flattered himself that his disavowal would still turn to the advantage of the Order. And indeed, the compassion he has excited until now is the reason why historians have not examined the trial documents with enough care. They should have remembered that the historian's first duty is the search for truth; a duty to which every other consideration must yield.

3°. *Voluntary confessions cannot constitute proof in matters that are incredible.*

*They believed that the Grand Master, the Visitors, and the Preceptors—who were often laymen—had the right to absolve them of their sins; as a result, they confessed only to the Brothers. They even believed that the Grand Master could grant them absolution without a prior confession. (1)*

The Leaders of the Order, it is true, often claim that they received this power from the Popes themselves; and for example, Brother William de Midleton, <note>A Scottish knight of the Order; the OCR "Ecoffois" reflects the archaic spelling of "Écossais."</note> asserts that the Grand Prior of England absolved them of their sins in the name of God, St. Peter, and the Pope. We see, therefore, that this assertion by the Templars is without foundation, and that they had taken it upon themselves to grant mutual forgiveness for all their sins.

<page-num>64</page-num>

...who hold the prejudice that the condemnation of the Templars had no real foundation, and that this great event was caused only by the greed of King Philip <note>Philip IV, known as Philip the Fair (reigned 1285–1314), who was heavily in debt to the Templars.</note>, incited by the leaders of the Order and by the Pope's <note>Pope Clement V, the first Avignon Pope, who was often seen as being under the influence of the French Crown.</note> servile compliance toward this Prince. If our last accusation alone were well-founded, the Templars were already very guilty according to the principles of the Court of Rome, which attach the remission of sins <note>The theological concept of being forgiven for sins through divine grace.</note> solely to the absolution of the priest, and exclude all seculars <note>In this context, "seculars" refers to laypeople—those who are not ordained members of the clergy.</note> from it. The Knights, who had never been legally confessed nor legally absolved, were excommunicated by that very fact; and these men further had the temerity to exempt themselves from this sentence by their own authority. Anyone who has maturely reflected on the astonishing influence of <term>auricular confession</term> <note>The practice of confessing sins privately to a priest to receive absolution, which was a cornerstone of Church authority and social control.</note> on the system of the ecclesiastical hierarchy will agree that if the Pope ever found a pressing motive to annihilate the Order, it must have been that they adopted and put into practice a principle which made them independent of all ecclesiastical power, and which, by spreading, could one day annihilate all the power of the Church itself.

<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the Templars' practice of granting each other absolution—a power reserved for priests—made them legally and theologically guilty in the eyes of the Church. This defiance of the ecclesiastical hierarchy provided the Pope with a significant motive to suppress the Order to protect the Church's spiritual monopoly.</summary>

...in our days, it has been possible to prove to the Jesuits—who likewise possessed the art of drawing great advantage from their confessors—that the Order indeed approved of maxims dangerous to Sovereigns, and that in times past, some of its members conformed their conduct to them; but as for the authority of the Church, they never attacked it, and yet the Court of Rome <note>The central administrative body of the Catholic Church, led by the Pope.</note> judged it useful and necessary to abolish them. How much more strongly must it [the Court of Rome] have been motivated, based on what we have just said, to destroy the Order of the Temple? That the extinction of this Order was carried out through torments and flames is something for which we must feel full horror, without, however, being surprised by it. This was in accordance with the laws of the Church and the spirit of those times. In 1212, one hundred people were burned in Strasbourg for having, in contempt of the Church, eaten meat during Lent <note>A 40-day period of fasting and penance preceding Easter in the Christian calendar.</note>, and for having disapproved of the celibacy of priests. <header>## ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS</header>

...sentiment of good and evil is so deeply imprinted in the heart of man, that no legislator, however limited he may be, could make a law evidently criminal, solely for the love of vice; such a law could at least not remain long in force. Therefore, when one finds it established in any society, it is necessary to look for its principle further afield. One will surely find it in the prejudices or the lusts <note>The original French *convoitifes* refers to strong desires or greed, often in a moral or carnal sense.</note> of men, or else one will see that the abuse was only introduced and tolerated to avoid something that was regarded, rightly or wrongly, as worse. The pretexts in question may have given rise to connivance <note>A "blind eye" or secret cooperation.</note>, but they are insufficient to explain a formal permission; they may have been used (and they were) by members of all possible Orders; but a legal permission for an entire body, that is something one will find nowhere.

I believe I have stumbled upon one of the reasons that may have prompted the **Superiors of the Order** to grant this singular permission. In the early times, there were **married Knights**. Ar-

Regarding a custom that seems so bizarre at first glance, it is appropriate to make the following observations.

It is very possible that some individuals favored abuses of the kind that the preceding accusation suggests. But in this case, one must not cast upon the entire Order the shame of a few isolated members—the unfortunate fruit of the total corruption of morals in that century of darkness <note>The "century of darkness" refers to the Middle Ages, which the author, writing from a later perspective, views as a period of moral and intellectual decline.</note>; a corruption of which history would provide us with striking examples, if it were necessary or appropriate to report them here.

However, the various confessions of the accused prove that these kisses, which seem indecent to us, were very common in the secret professions <note>The "secret professions" refer to the private, higher-level initiation ceremonies of the Order that were not open to the public.</note>, so that they must not be attributed solely to a few individuals. Here are my conjectures on this matter.

The reader will recall that the Order of the Temple had several different professions; beginning with the second, they all became very secret:

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that the secret and "indecent" rituals of the Templars were not evidence of heresy, but rather a form of institutional gatekeeping common to medieval guilds.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>

<sig>D 3</sig>

It is perfectly natural that the leaders never admitted a large portion of the Knights, whether because they could not rely on their discretion, or for other reasons; that the latter knew something secret was being practiced within the Order and desired to be admitted is also quite natural. To rid themselves of these persistent requests, it was therefore necessary to attach conditions to this knowledge that were unpleasant enough to discourage them. It is well known that during the establishment of the Corporations <note>The author uses the term "Corporations" to refer to medieval professional guilds or trade associations.</note> during the Middle Ages, initiation ceremonies were loaded with unpleasant, ridiculous, and indecent rituals to prevent the number of aspirants from growing too large. We are even more specifically aware of the details of the cruelties, combined with indecencies, which—during the time of the famous *Northern Hanse* <note>The Hanseatic League (Northern Hanse) was a powerful commercial and defensive confederation of merchant guilds and market towns in Central and Northern Europe.</note> (1)—

---

at which this man became terrified, turned pale, and told him that if he loved his own life, he must never speak of this to his Superiors.

These clandestine assemblies had, from that time, inspired several people with prejudices unfavorable to the Order (1), and this was quite natural, since such mysteries were neither in use in other bodies, nor justified by the very rule of the Templars; moreover, it was very apparent that they had not been introduced without reason; and one can surely conclude from this that the practice of holding <term>Chapters</term> <note>The "Chapter" was the formal assembly of the members of a religious or military order to discuss business and discipline.</note> behind closed doors was only introduced for some hidden reason which did not exist at the beginning. (1) In Scotland fifteen people testify: *That they know nothing to say against the persons of the said Brothers of the said Order, neither concerning their reception nor their profession of vows, because they never saw anyone in Scotland or elsewhere being received or even taking vows as a Brother: because they always did this clandestinely. Because of this, both they and their ancestors had held a poor opinion of the aforementioned Order and its Brothers. And especially so since they saw other religious orders receive members and take vows publicly, and in their receptions and professions, friends, parents, and neighbors were invited.* <note>original Latin: "Quod contra personas dictorum Fratrum... amicos, parentes & vicinos vocari."</note> Du Puy, p. 532.

<meta>catchword visible through paper; bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

the fourth section, intended to deal with the accusation that follows, will show what this reason was, and that the Templars did well to keep it secret. Historians—repulsed by the harshness so common in the fourteenth century <note>The author refers to the early 1300s, specifically the arrests in 1307 and the subsequent trials and executions that ended the Order.</note> among both Kings and the Clergy with which the Templars were treated—have allowed themselves to be swayed into finding the Templars entirely innocent and their judges entirely unjust. But should they not have considered that the thick veil with which they covered their assemblies ought to inspire great suspicion against them? Such a mystery could not have been the result of mere chance; it is, therefore, doubly necessary to examine these secrets closely, rather than glossing over this matter as all previous historians have done.

### SIXTH ACCUSATION.

1st. *They were required, at the secret reception <note>The formal initiation ceremony for new members into the Order.</note>, to renounce Jesus Christ and to trample upon the Cross (1).*
2nd. *They did not use the formula of Consecration (2) <note>The specific words spoken by a priest during Mass to transform bread and wine into the Body and Blood of Christ. Omitting this would render the sacrament "invalid" in the eyes of the Church.</note>, when they received communion.*
3rd. *They displayed a certain effigy in the General Chapter <note>An official assembly of the high-ranking members of the Order.</note> and worshipped it (3).*
4th. *On this occasion, they were given a belt that was said to have touched the Idol (4).*

I group these four accusations into one, because they relate to one another, and because by examining them in depth in the following section, they will serve to explain each other.

We have seen above that these accusations were only confessed by a certain number of Knights, and we have shown the reason for this, these customs being properly the *Disciplina* <note>The text ends mid-sentence at "Disciplina," likely referring to the Latin term "Disciplina Arcani" or "Discipline of the Secret," a practice of hiding certain rites from the uninitiated.</note>

---

(1) See the 123 articles, Nos. 1-13. <note>These "articles" refer to the specific list of charges drawn up by the inquisitors during the trial of the Templars.</note>
(2) Nos. 16-18.
(3) Nos. 14-53.
(4) Nos. 54-57.

<header>FILED AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.</header>

*Secrets* <note>original: "Arcani"</note> of the Order, which were only shared with a few elite Knights during one or several secret initiations <note>The formal ceremony of induction into the Order, known as a "reception."</note>.

These accusations are serious, and I admit that at first glance they appear strange; however, their reality is confirmed in so many different places, by so many voluntary and consistent testimonies, that after having maturely examined and compared them, one must be persuaded that they were neither fabricated nor forced (1). They certainly deserve a 

---

(1) This is what several historians have liked to persuade themselves of without the slightest likelihood; and this would have been impossible for them had they compared the reports of the Templars from different countries. The author of the history of the Templars, written in French, has only cast a passing glance upon this, and yet he says, at least regarding the French Knights: "That it is beyond all likelihood that, to please the King, they would have wished to dishonor their Order and imagine crimes; that one must therefore in no way doubt the truth of their confessions." But his German translator, who made no research into this, as is clearly seen, formally contradicts his author in his uninformative remarks; he says bluntly that all the confessions of the criminals are false or forced, and that their great wealth was their greatest crime.

<header>ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS</header>

### SIXTH ACCUSATION.

1. *They were obliged, during the secret reception <note>The formal ceremony of induction into the Order, often held at night and behind closed doors.</note>, to renounce Jesus Christ & to step upon the Cross* (1).

2. *They did not use the formula of Consecration* (2) <note>The specific liturgical words spoken by a priest to transform the bread and wine into the Eucharist.</note>, *when they received communion.*

3. *In the General Chapter <note>The administrative and legislative assembly of the Order’s high-ranking members.</note>, they displayed a certain effigy and worshipped it* (3).

4. *On this occasion, they were given a cord <note>original: "ceinture"</note> which was said to have touched the Idol* (4).

I gather these four accusations into one, because they relate to each other, and by examining them thoroughly in the following section, they will serve to explain one another.

We have seen above that these accusations were only admitted by a certain number of Knights, and we have shown the reason for this: these customs being strictly the *Secret Discipline* <note>original: "Disciplina [Arcani]"; a historical practice of keeping certain religious rites hidden from the public or the uninitiated.</note>

---

(1) See the 123 articles, Nos. 1-13.
(2) Nos. 16-18.
(3) Nos. 14-53.
(4) Nos. 54-57.

Secrets <note>original: "Arcani"; referring to the secret knowledge or hidden mysteries of the Order.</note> of the Order, which were shared only with a few select Knights during one or more secret receptions.

These accusations are grave, and I admit that at first glance they appear strange; however, their reality is confirmed in so many different places, by so many voluntary and consistent testimonies, that after having maturely examined and compared them, one must be convinced that they were neither invented nor forced (1). They certainly deserve a

---

(1) This is what several historians have liked to convince themselves of without the slightest probability; and this would have been impossible for them if they had compared the reports of the Templars from different countries. But his German translator, who clearly did no research on the matter, as is evident, formally contradicts his author in his uninformative remarks; he says bluntly that all the confessions <note>original: "aveux"</note> of the criminals are false or forced, and that their vast wealth was their greatest crime.

Most of those from the French <note>The author refers here to the French Knights Templar who were interrogated during the trials led by Philip the Fair.</note> are incomplete, such that one must seek in one what is missing in another; however, although they diverge in the minor details, they agree on the substance. Brother Robert de Supervillam of Ifis sent his nephew to Rome, during the Year of the Jubilee <note>The Jubilee of 1300, a holy year of pilgrimage and amnesty declared by Pope Boniface VIII.</note>, to obtain absolution <note>A formal release from guilt or sin granted by the Church.</note> for it; but since the nephew died on the way, he took no further steps to obtain it (1). 4°. Finally, the Belt was the mark of Knighthood. The Templars received one during their public reception. That they might have wanted to use them for magical operations is something for which not a shadow of probability will be found in all the gathered testimonies. ## SECTION IV.

### *Detailed examination of the custom adopted within the Order of the Temple of renouncing Jesus Christ, and of the name Baphemetus, with everything pertaining to this subject.*

That the Inquisitors <note>The Papal and Royal officials tasked with identifying and punishing heresy during the trials of the Templars (1307–1312).</note>, as soon as they had obtained the appearance of a confession regarding all these accusations, should hasten to condemn the unfortunate Templars without further examination—be it to public penance <term>amende honorable|a humiliating ritual of public confession and request for forgiveness, often involving the prisoner being stripped to their shirt and holding a torch</term>, to perpetual imprisonment, or even to the flames—is something very much in keeping with the spirit of those times, when one was far more concerned with condemning and punishing Heresy than with investigating its principles; for what, indeed, would...

<meta>This page continues the sentence from the bottom of page 102, completing the author's thought on why the legal and religious environment of the 14th century suppressed genuine inquiry into the Templars' beliefs.</meta>

<header>BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.</header>

...have served, since the death penalty was attached to any opinion that strayed in the slightest degree from those things that the Church <note>The Roman Catholic Church, which held supreme legal authority over matters of faith in the Middle Ages.</note> commanded one to believe, and since Heretics were only pardoned by a very special grace and after having been made to sign a profession of faith? These professions and the pyres <note>original: "buchers" — the wooden stakes where those convicted of heresy were burned alive.</note> are great obstacles to any research; both are causes of the obscurity that shrouds the history of Heresies, and especially the mysteries of our Templars. As soon as Heresy was perceived, reasoning ceased; for in order to condemn one to the flames, one did not look for one error more or less.

But there is reason to be astonished that after everything that has been written about the Templars, no one has undertaken to untangle their history. A very just and striking horror has served as a pretext for everyone to avoid a discussion that is certainly embarrassing; people have contented themselves with crying out in horror and impossibility, without reflecting that if incontestable testimonies prove the reality of an improbable fact, there must exist a point of view from which it will be better perceived. Some months ago, while reading the confessions of these religious men <note>The Templars were a military-monastic order; though soldiers, they took the same vows as monks.</note> with attention for the first time, I was struck by several circumstances capable of explaining this matter, and I was very surprised to see that no one had made use of them: in pursuing my research, I have endeavored to shed as much light as possible on such obscure facts.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the accusations against the Knights Templar, specifically examining the nature of the alleged denial of Christ during their secret initiation rituals. It follows the argument that these actions were not merely random acts of malice, but may have had a specific theological intent.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

We see through the detailed testimony of many that this command to deny Jesus Christ was accompanied by that of believing in an Almighty God, Creator of Heaven and Earth (1).

It is evident, therefore, that this was a matter of neither mockery nor malice, but rather a desire to deny the Divinity of Jesus Christ and to establish the Unity of God (2).

---

(1) Brother Jean de Stoke: Questioned as a witness as to who the Master told him he should believe in once he had denied Jesus Christ, he responded, "in the Great Almighty God who created Heaven and Earth, and not in the Crucified one" <note>original Latin: "in Magnum Deum Omnipotentem qui creavit Cœlum & Terram, & non in Crucifixum." The "Crucified one" refers to the human form of Jesus on the cross.</note>. Du Puy, p. 399. Brother Thomas de Toccy of Thoroldeby: "that the said Guy, the Grand Master <note>original Latin: "Guido Magnus Magister" — likely referring to Guy de la Tour, a prominent Templar official.</note>, dogmatized him that he should believe in the great God: and enjoined the same that he should remain in the society of the good Men of the Order," p. 396. Brother Jean de Cassanhas: the Preceptor <note>A "Preceptor" was a high-ranking officer in charge of a Templar house or "Preceptory."</note> said to him, "you must promise God and us — that you believe in God the Creator, who has not died and will not die," p. 215. Some, in truth, had not understood this very well, as can be seen by the testimony of Brother Stephen of Stapelbrugge, who said: (See du Puy, p. 393) "I do not know in whom they ought to believe, unless it be in an evil spirit" <note>original Latin: "Nescio in quem credere deberent, nisi in malignum spiritum."</note>. But he is the only one who speaks thus, and he betrays his simplicity by adding that in every Chapter a Knight was lost (that is to say, that the Devil carried him away).

(2) "It is necessary for you to deny that Jesus Christ is true"

But we know that the Unity of God is the great dogma of the Mohammedan <note>A historical term for Islamic</note> Religion. The Saracens <note>A term used by medieval Europeans to describe Muslim Arabs and Turks</note>, for whom the mystery of the Trinity was incomprehensible and the adoration of Images scandalous, reproached the Christians for having several Gods, and did not hesitate to call them Idolaters, (1) slaves of the Cross, while they themselves took the title of Unitarians <note>referring to the Islamic doctrine of *Tawhid*, or the Oneness of God</note> (2). During the Middle Ages, Christians and even many members of the clergy had only very vague ideas about the Trinity (1). The Dogma of the Divinity and the two Natures of Jesus Christ, so closely linked with that of the Trinity, had produced, since the introduction of the Athanasian Creed <note>A statement of Christian belief focused on Trinitarian doctrine and Christology, attributed to St. Athanasius</note>, a multitude of opinions, controversies, and sects. Each person wished to explain in his own way the mysteries of the two Natures, Divine and Human; it was in this way that there arose

<page-num>110</page-num>
# ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS #

the sects of the Monophysites <note>A group that believed Christ had only one divine nature, rather than being both fully human and fully divine</note>, the Adoptionists <note>Those who held that Jesus was born human and only became the "Son of God" by adoption at his baptism</note>, and others. These were soon joined by the Gnostics and the Manicheans, who, modifying and changing their systems from generation to generation, sought at all costs to clarify the crude ideas held regarding the two natures of our Lord. They did this through certain poorly understood principles of modern Platonism <note>Neoplatonism, a philosophical system that attempted to reconcile Greek philosophy with mystical concepts of the "One"</note>; all of these were sectarians who rejected the Divinity of Jesus. The ruling Church did indeed excommunicate these heresies; but such proceedings do not destroy opinions; they only cause them to be hidden. They subsist in secret and spread considerably as soon as they find a principle in the human spirit that favors them. The ecclesiastical history of every century provides us with convincing proof of this.

One finds particularly in the history of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries several attempts to enlighten and fix the Mystery of the Trinity, and it is remarkable that these efforts almost always ended in ideas of unity <note>The concept of God as a single, undivided being, often at the expense of the three-person doctrine of the Trinity</note>, which were not long in being anathematized <note>Formally denounced and excommunicated by the Church</note>. Cathars <note>A dualist medieval religious sect that flourished in Southern France; they believed the physical world was evil and thus rejected Christ's literal incarnation</note>, who only recognized Jesus as a created being, and the Albigensians <note>A branch of the Cathar movement named after the city of Albi; they were the targets of a crusade in the 13th century</note>, who certainly did not believe him to be God. It is remarkable that the origin of the philosophical sect of the Nominalists <term>Nominalists: A school of thought that argued universal concepts (like "humanity") are just names and do not exist as real entities, which had profound implications for how the three "persons" of the Trinity were understood</term>, which arose in the eleventh century in the University of Paris, was at the same time that of the principles of Unity <note>Referencing Unitarianism, or the belief that God is a single entity rather than a Trinity</note>, which spread through Europe at that time.

Roscelin, leader of these philosophers, maintained that by admitting the Trinity, one had to admit three Gods, since without this, God the Father and the Holy Spirit would have had to become incarnate with the Son. He was condemned. But his disciple Abelard <note>Peter Abelard (1079–1142), a famous philosopher and theologian known for his tragic romance with Héloïse and his controversial intellectual methods</note> explained himself much more clearly still in his book *On the Trinity* <note>original: *de Trinitate*</note>, and maintained: "That God the Father alone is almighty." He was refuted according to the fashion of that century—that is to say, the Council of Soissons forced him to throw his own book into the fire, to recite the Athanasian Creed <note>original: *symbole d’Athanaſe*; a Christian statement of belief focusing on Trinitarian doctrine and Christology</note> (1) and to go into retreat in the Abbey of St. Médard. The Bishop of Chartres finally wished to defend him. His violent adversaries, Albéric and Lodulphe, weak disciples of the very—

### ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS

<meta>catchword: ranconnoit</meta>

These nations became so familiar with one another that they held tournaments together, the Christians danced to the sound of Saracen <note>An archaic European term for Muslims during the Middle Ages</note> instruments, and the latter took pleasure in the songs of the Christians (1). The proud and choleric Pope Gregory IX entered into correspondence with the Sultan of Egypt, with the aim of ruining Emperor Frederick II <note>Holy Roman Emperor (1194–1250) who was frequently at odds with the Papacy and led the Sixth Crusade</note>. Pope Innocent IV, on the contrary, forbade Christians by a Bull of 1253 from minting coins with the image of Mohammed, a practice which, consequently, must have become quite common. The Templars granted the Saracens the freedom of their worship (2). The Order, as much as Emperor Frederick II and King Richard of England <note>Richard the Lionheart</note>, concluded alliances with them at different times: prisoners were well-treated on both sides; sometimes they were ransomed

---

"between the Christians and Saracens they seemed to be as if they were neither," <note>original: "inter Christianos & Saracenes tanquam quidem neutri esse videbantur"</note> says Du Cange <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a preeminent scholar of the Middle Ages</note>, under the word *Pullani* <note>A term for the descendants of Crusaders who settled in the Levant</note>. Jacques de Vitry <note>A 13th-century historian and Bishop of Acre</note> gives an even less favorable account of them.

(1) Marin, *History of Saladin*, Volume II, p. 146.

(2) See Matthew Paris <note>A famous 13th-century English chronicler and monk</note>, p. 547.

ransomed, and often they were sent back without ransom. The Christians finally convinced themselves that the Saracens <note>An archaic term for Muslims during the Middle Ages</note>, whom they had initially reduced to the level of brutes, were a generous and even enlightened people. Saladin <note>Salah ad-Din (1137–1193), the Sultan who led the Muslim military campaign against the Crusader states</note> earned general esteem. It is said that he had himself initiated by a captive Knight, Hugh of Tiberias <note>Hugh II of Saint Omer (died 1204), a prominent noble in the Kingdom of Jerusalem</note>, into all the customs of the Order, with the exception of the religious profession; and this anecdote (1), whether true or false, proves at least that the two nations observed each other with great attention.

Taking all these considerations together, it seems neither strange nor improbable that a Templar, upon returning from captivity, might have confided to his brothers that the Saracens believed in only *one God*; a discovery that would have necessarily excited their attention.
