<page-num>122</page-num>

# ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS

...[a] secret rule of the Order; it is not apparent that for the love of the Saracens <note>A term used by medieval Christians to describe Muslims</note> the Templars would have decided to renounce Jesus, the Savior of all Christians, and to introduce into their Order a new *secret profession*.

History still leaves us with traces that explain the true circumstances of this singular fact. The Templars kept an *image*, which took the form of a human head, and which they only displayed and worshiped in their most secret assemblies. Is it believable that the worship of this idol was transmitted to them by Muslims <note>Original: "Mahométans"</note>, a people who held both images (1) and their veneration in horror? We must therefore look for another origin for the worship offered to this head. The name alone that it bore can provide us with some clues. It was called: a bearded idol, made *in the figure of Baphomet* <note>Original Latin: "in figuram Baſſometi"</note>; or, what is even clearer, *an idol where the figure of Baphomet was depicted* <note>Original Latin: "ubi erat depicta figura Baſſometi"</note>.

***

(1) In the 13th Century, all sculptors in Valencia were forbidden from working publicly on images, because of the Moors <note>The Muslim inhabitants of the Iberian Peninsula</note> who were scandalized by them. See the *Ecclesiastical History* of Semler <note>Johann Salomo Semler (1725–1791), a German theologian and pioneer of the historical-critical method</note>, based on the *Annals* of Waddingius <note>Luke Wadding (1588–1657), an Irish Franciscan friar and historian who wrote a history of his Order</note>.

<summary>The author argues that the Templars' alleged worship of a bearded head (Baphomet) could not have been a practice adopted from Islam, as Muslims strictly forbid religious imagery. He suggests the name of the idol itself holds the key to its true origin.</summary>
<keywords>Baphomet, idolatry, Knights Templar, iconoclasm, Moors, Johann Salomo Semler, Luke Wadding, Saracens</keywords>

This word even escaped the learned research of du Cange <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a preeminent French philologist and historian known for his authoritative dictionaries of medieval Latin and Greek</note>. Although I was convinced beforehand that this Idol could never have come from the Saracens <note>A term used by medieval Europeans for Muslims</note>, I nevertheless consulted, for greater certainty, Professor Eichhorn of Jena <note>Johann Gottfried Eichhorn (1752–1827), a German theologian and pioneer in the study of Oriental languages and biblical criticism</note>, one of our connoisseurs of Oriental languages, and he confirmed for me that the word *Baphomet* could not pass for Arabic in any natural sense.

Another etymology presents itself, which at first appears simple enough: Mahomet <note>The name used in this period for Muhammad, the prophet of Islam</note> can also be pronounced Bahomet, since in Oriental languages the letter *m* is often changed to *b* (1). <meta>This page continues the philological investigation into the name "Baphomet." Having established in the previous page that "Mahomet" (Muhammad) was sometimes spelled "Bahomet" due to phonetic shifts, the author now addresses why this still doesn't quite explain the specific spelling of "Baphomet" used in the Templar trials.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>
<header>ESSAY ON THE ACCUSATIONS</header>

Among the Arabs, the name of the Prophet is nowhere written as either *Bahomet* or *Bahumet*; but Latin historians of the <term>Crusades</term> <note>The series of religious wars between Christians and Muslims in the medieval period.</note> have sometimes spelled it this way (1). On the other hand, the nature of the Arabic language allows even less for the changing of the *ch* or *h* into *f* or *ph*—that is to say, *Bahomet* into *Baphomet*; however, it so happens that a Latin historian used the name *Baphomet* a single time to refer to the Prophet (2); and this sole authority could give rise to the idea that the figure of *Baffomet* worshipped by the <term>Templars</term> <note>The Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, a wealthy and powerful Catholic military order suppressed in the early 14th century.</note> was the image of <term>Mahomet</term> <note>A medieval European corruption of the name of the Prophet Muhammad.</note>.

---

(1) For example, in <term>Raymond of Aguilers'</term> <note>A chronicler of the First Crusade who wrote an eyewitness account of the siege of Jerusalem.</note> *History of Jerusalem*: "If he should come against us in battle, [let them] worship Alim, whom he himself worships, who is of the lineage of Bahumeth." <note>Original Latin: *Si veniret contra nos in prælium, & colorent Alim, quem ipse colit, qui est de genere Bahumeth.*</note> See also *The Deeds of God through the Franks*, pp. 164 & 165. <note>Original title: *Gesta Dei per Francos*, a major 17th-century compilation of primary sources from the Crusades.</note>

(2) See the *Letter of Anselm of Ribemont to Manasses, Archbishop of Reims*, from the year 1099. <note>Anselm of Ribemont was a French count and crusader; this letter is a primary source for the siege of Antioch.</note> It is included in <term>D'Achery's Spicilegium</term>, Volume II, folio p. 431. <note>A vast collection of rare historical documents compiled by the Benedictine scholar Luc d'Achery (1609–1685).</note> "We, however, having gone out against them, were defeated and put to flight. They indeed entered the walls with us; throughout that day and the following night we remained there together, separated from one another by barely a stone's throw. On the following day, as dawn appeared, they called upon Baphomet with loud voices; and we, praying to our God in our hearts, made an attack upon them and expelled them all from the city walls." <note>Original Latin: *Nos autem contra illos egressi victi sumus atque fugati. Ipsi vero nobiscum muros ingressi illum diem & noctem sequentem insimul fuimus destantes, ab invicem quasi uno lapidis ictio. Sequenti die, aurora apparente altis vocibus Baphomet invocaverunt; & nos Deum nostrum in cordibus nostris deprecantes impetum fecimus in eos, de muris civitatis omnes expulimus.*</note>

Be that as it may, I cannot convince myself of it. What if this word *Baphomet*, which appears only a single time in this sense, is nothing more than a printing error for *Bahomet*? I doubt, moreover, that during the <term>Middle Ages</term> the Latin word *figura* <note>original Latin: "figure" or "form"</note> was understood to mean a physical image; it properly signified a *sign* or symbol. But above all, what immense difficulties would have been encountered by a <term>Religious Knight</term> <note>The Templars were "Religious Knights" because they took monastic vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience while serving as soldiers.</note> who wished to introduce the secret worship of <term>Mahomet</term> <note>A medieval European corruption of the name of the Prophet Muhammad.</note> into his Order? What could have possibly prompted the <term>Templars</term> to secretly embrace the religion of their enemies?

Even if one were to accept all these suppositions, the confusion would only increase. The <term>Muslims</term> <note>The original French uses "Mahométans."</note> hold the worship of images in horror <note>A reference to aniconism in Islam, which strictly forbids the use of idols or representative images in worship.</note>, and yet it is claimed that the Templars possessed an image of Mahomet which they worshipped in secret? Perhaps it will be argued that in the public exercise of their religion, images were regarded as symbols of their faith, and that following this same principle, they had also made an image of Mahomet to worship in secret? But one only has to remember that in their <term>receptions</term> <note>The formal, often private, initiation ceremonies where new members were admitted into the Order.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the "Baphomet" accusation is logically flawed, noting that Muslims—whom the Templars were accused of imitating—strictly forbid the use of idols or images in worship.</summary>

emerged almost at the same time as Christianity. <term>Neoplatonism</term> <note>A philosophical system, popular in the early centuries AD, that combined Plato's ideas with oriental mysticism.</note> was already in vogue among the Jews in the first century after Jesus Christ, and it soon produced the dogmas of the <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>A school of thought in Jewish mysticism; the word literally means "received tradition."</note>, which could have led to a good and sound philosophy were it not for the enigmatic language that shrouded them, and to which the most unjust and absurd interpretations were subsequently given. The result was a multitude of fancies, which have been preserved until our day among the Jews in various countries, and which often still deceive the credulity of Christians.

The Kabbalistic philosophy was wise and respectable in its origin. It taught, for example, that the <term>Ein Sof</term> <note>From the Hebrew "En Sof," meaning "infinite" or "without end."</note>, or the infinite God, is a being incomprehensible to the human mind, and for that reason His name must not be pronounced (1). They called them *Numbers* or <term>Sephirot</term> <note>In the Kabbalah, the ten *Sephirot* are the emanations or attributes through which the Infinite God reveals Himself and creates the universe.</note>, and as the various sects of the <term>Gnostics</term> <note>Early mystical religious groups who emphasized personal spiritual knowledge (gnosis) over orthodox teachings.</note> refined this doctrine, they were turned into *Intellectual Powers* <note>original Greek: "δυναμεις" (dynameis)</note>, *Princes* <note>original Greek: "αρχοντες" (archontes), often referred to in English as Archons; these were seen as celestial rulers of the material world.</note>, and eventually *Angels*. The first <term>Kabbalists</term> <note>Followers of Jewish mysticism.</note> certainly held as a principle that God, considered in Himself, being an immutable being, self-sufficient, and known only to Himself in the full extent of His infinite and ineffable Divinity, could not be understood by finite beings; they held that this infinite God was known to men only through creation and through the divine attributes that manifest themselves in creation. ...also had an allegorical figure, which explains these same ideas: it represented God, taken in an *abstract* sense, in the form of a *beardless head*; and the *Creator God* in the form of a *bearded head* (1). The former was the symbol of immutability; the latter was intended to be the image of a Creation, which constantly renews itself throughout all of nature.

The <term>Gnostics</term> <note>A collection of early mystical religious groups that emphasized personal spiritual knowledge over orthodox church teachings.</note> then emerged from the <term>Kabbalists</term> <note>Followers of the Jewish mystical tradition known as the Kabbalah.</note>. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic sects and their ritual practices. While the previous page focused on the Ophites and Basilidians' denial of the physical crucifixion, this page explores the Carpocratians and the transition from Jewish mystical metaphors into the creation of physical Gnostic idols.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>BROUGHT AGAINST THE TEMPLARS.</header>

The *Carpocratians* <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic sect from Alexandria that believed in the transmigration of souls and sought to return to a primordial unity.</note> secretly performed a type of pagan worship toward images of Jesus Christ, which they paired with those of Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle.

Everything that was not Christian or Jewish was considered "Pagan" by the <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>The influential early Christian theologians whose writings established the foundations of the Church.</note>. Since it is almost impossible for them to have had an exact knowledge of these secret images, it is safest to believe in general that the Carpocratians, the *Basilidians*, and other Gnostics did indeed possess images, though one cannot say precisely what they represented. To better understand this, one must remember that the <term>Kabbalists</term> <note>Jewish mystics who studied the hidden, symbolic meanings of the Torah.</note>, who followed the Judaic Law, were forbidden from making or worshiping images; however, they permitted themselves allegories and figurative expressions in their language, many of which were borrowed from the human body, the face, the beard, the differences between the sexes, and generation <note>the concept of divine procreation or the emanating of spiritual beings.</note>. The Gnostics, who were their immediate successors, having abandoned the Judaic Law, extended this freedom to the point of creating *images*, turning the allegories that were accepted in their philosophy into physical reality.

<summary>The author explains how the Carpocratians and other Gnostic groups used physical images in their worship, arguing that these idols were physical realizations of the symbolic and anatomical allegories originally used by Jewish Kabbalists.</summary>
<keywords>Carpocratians, Gnosticism, Kabbalah, Church Fathers, idols, religious symbolism, Judaic Law, religious philosophy</keywords>

The *Carpocratians* <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic sect founded by Carpocrates of Alexandria, known for their belief in reaching salvation through experiencing all human experiences and their use of icons.</note> secretly rendered a sort of pagan worship to images of Jesus Christ, which they associated with those of Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle.

Everything that was not Christian or Jewish was considered Pagan by the <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>The influential early Christian theologians and writers who established the intellectual and doctrinal foundations of Christianity.</note>. Since it is almost impossible for them to have had exact knowledge of these secret images, it is safest to believe in general that the Carpocratians, the Basilidians, and other Gnostics did indeed possess images, though we cannot say for certain what they represented. To better understand this, we must remember that it was forbidden for the Cabalists, who followed the Jewish Law, to make and worship images; but they allowed themselves allegories and figurative expressions in their language, borrowing many of these from the human body, the face, the beard, the difference between the sexes, and generation <note>In Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah), divine attributes are often described using human anatomical metaphors, such as the "Long Face" (Arich Anpin) or the "Beard" of God, to represent different aspects of the divine flow.</note>. The Gnostics, their immediate successors, having abandoned the Jewish Law, extended this freedom to the point of *making images for themselves*, materializing the allegories that were accepted in their philosophy.

<summary>This page discusses how Gnostic sects like the Carpocratians moved from the abstract anatomical metaphors of Jewish Kabbalah to the creation of physical cult images, blending Christian figures with Greek philosophers.</summary>

Basilides <note>An early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria (c. 117–138 AD) who claimed to have received secret teachings from the Apostle Matthias.</note> imposed a silence of several years upon his disciples (1). When they were fully initiated into his **knowledge** <note>original: *gnosis*</note>, he attributed to them an **election** <note>original: *eklogē* — the concept of being chosen for salvation</note>, which he set in opposition to the **temporal world** <note>original: *ta kosmika* — things belonging to the physical universe</note> (2). Marcion <note>A 2nd-century theologian whose followers, the Marcionites, rejected the Old Testament and believed the God of the Jews was a lesser deity than the God of Jesus.</note> and other Gnostics proudly raised their followers, the **spiritual ones** <note>original: *pneumatikoi* — those who possess a "divine spark" of spirit</note>, above human nature, the **carnal ones** <note>original: *psychikoi* — those who only possess a soul and are bound to the material world</note>; and Basilides, supported by his **knowledge**, placed only those of his sect in the rank of men, and he cast all the rest into the class of animals (3). He composed from the essence of his **knowledge** a secret discipline, which was meant to be known by only one in a thousand, and two in ten thousand. He gave this precept to his disciples: *you must know everything, but no one shall know you* (4). Priscillian <note>A 4th-century bishop from Roman Spain who was the first person in the history of Christianity to be executed for heresy; his followers were known for their asceticism and alleged secrecy.</note> also had his mysteries. *Swear*, he said to his disciples, *swear falsely, but do not betray our secrets* (5). Mr. de Beaufobre correctly observes (1) that this collection was made without the slightest selection <note>original: "sans le moindre choix" — implying the collection lacked critical discernment or curation.</note>. He goes too far, however, in maintaining that not a single one of these stones belongs to Basilides <note>Basilides was a prominent Gnostic teacher in Alexandria during the 2nd century AD.</note>. Some are obviously of Gnostic origin; and this origin should suffice, whether they date back to the time of Basilides or not. There are two which seemed particularly remarkable to me (2), and I have had them copied in the engraving at the beginning of this treatise (fig. 1, 2). Chiflet claims, and I believe his conjecture is well-founded, that they represent the *Supreme Father of the Gnostics*,

---

(1) The following example will prove that Mr. de Beaufobre did not pay enough attention to these antiquities, which, in truth, are only a supplementary part of his grand and excellent work <note>Isaac de Beaufobre (1659–1738) was a French Protestant theologian famous for his massive study on Manichaeism and early church heresies.</note>. After having asserted that none of these engraved stones could belong to Basilides, he nonetheless makes an exception for No. 90 on page 60, and at the same time falls into a most singular misunderstanding. He believes this piece to be authentic because it represents *a man attempting to carry a sheep on his shoulders* <note>The "Good Shepherd" was a common symbol in early Christian art; Beaufobre likely thought this confirmed a Christian-Gnostic link.</note>. Firstly, the conclusion would not be correct; but what is even more astonishing is that Mr. de Beaufobre was mistaken about the figures; there is no sheep on the antiquity in question, but rather one sees *a man making efforts to strangle a lion* <note>This likely depicts the mythological labor of Hercules with the Nemean Lion, a common motif that the author argues Beaufobre misidentified.</note>.

(2) In Macarius they are numbered 77 and 78 <note>Referring to the catalog of gems in the work *Abraxas* by Jean l’Heureux (Joannes Macarius).</note>.

Until then, the opinions of Christians regarding the two natures of Jesus Christ <term>two natures</term> <note>This refers to the "Hypostatic Union," the theological debate over how Jesus could be simultaneously fully human and fully divine.</note> were still very much divided. The Manichaeans, the Monophysites, the Adoptionists, the Cathars, and the Bogomils <note>These were various religious groups labeled as heretics by the medieval Church; they often held dualist views or disagreed with the standard definition of the Trinity.</note> prove to us well enough how much this subject was meditated upon, and under how many forms it was reproduced by the philosophy then dominant. The principles of the Gnostics <note>A diverse group of early mystical religions that emphasized "gnosis" or secret spiritual knowledge.</note> were more or less spread everywhere. The Templars, therefore, were bound soon to encounter people who, in the manner of the Gnostics, admitted the Unity of God, and whose ideas on the Divinity of Jesus Christ and the mystery of the Trinity differed from the dogmas of the dominant Church. This doctrine had always been a secret discipline <term>secret discipline</term> <note>From the Latin *disciplina arcani*, the historical practice of keeping certain religious teachings hidden from those outside the faith.</note> among the Gnostics; it had to be so more than ever in a time when at the slightest suspicion it was punished by torture <note>The original French *la queſtion* refers specifically to judicial torture used to extract information or confessions.</note> and the stake; consequently, it also became a secret discipline for the Templars when they adopted it. Now I believe I have sufficiently developed everything belonging to my subject. It seems to me that based on my ideas one can easily reconcile all the known details of the Order of the Templars; and that, far from being unbelievable, they are explained quite naturally, however strange they might appear.

The idea of a secret philosophy existed among the Gnostic sects <note>Gnosticism was a collection of early Christian-era religious groups who claimed "gnosis" or secret knowledge of the divine</note>; we find it again among the Templars. The baptism of the **Mind** <note>original Greek: <term>νους</term> (nous); representing the divine intellect</note> and of the **Spirit** <note>original Greek: <term>πνευμα</term> (pneuma); representing the holy breath or essence</note> bears a strong resemblance to that of **Wisdom** <note>original Greek: <term>μητος</term> (metos/metis); often associated in these texts with a ritual baptism into Gnostic wisdom</note>, and even the Templars' custom of introducing their initiates <note>original French: *profès*; those who have taken their vows</note> stripped down to their shirts seems to correspond to the idea of a baptism. The belt they received at their secret profession, which they wore over their shirt, was the sign of a new, but secret, Knighthood (1). The image of the Creator, used among the Gnostics, was adopted by the Templars

---

The denial of <term>Jesus Christ</term> <note>The author refers here to the specific accusation that Templars were required to deny Christ during their secret initiation</note>, as astonishing as it may seem, was also a custom borrowed from the <term>Gnostics</term> <note>Gnosticism was a collection of ancient religious ideas that emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) over orthodox faith</note>, as was the contempt for the Cross. The <term>Gnostics</term> did not believe at all in a Redemption brought about by blood; they did not believe that <term>Jesus Christ</term> had taken a physical body <note>This belief is known as Docetism—the idea that Christ’s physical body and crucifixion were merely appearances or illusions</note>: therefore they could not believe that they received his body in the <term>Eucharist</term> <note>The Christian ceremony of Holy Communion, where bread and wine are consecrated as the body and blood of Christ</note>; and this dogma was also that of the <term>Templars</term> <note>A wealthy and powerful Catholic military order suppressed in the early 14th century</note>, who for this reason omitted the words of consecration and believed they were receiving only a simple host <note>The unleavened bread used in the mass; the author suggests they viewed it as a mere symbol rather than a miracle</note>.

But, lest anyone imagine that these sorts of dogmas were only in vogue in the early times of the <term>Gnostics</term>, I shall cite several authorities which prove that the same opinions still subsisted elsewhere in the time of the <term>Templars</term>. At the same period when the <term>Templars</term> were before the <term>Inquisition</term> <note>The judicial system of the Catholic Church used to identify and suppress heresy</note>, Etienne de Proaudo (1), of the Sect of the

<term>Albigensian</term> <note>The Albigensians were a religious sect in Southern France during the 12th and 13th centuries, persecuted by the Catholic Church for their dualist beliefs</note>, was brought, in 1307, before (1) the <term>Inquisitor of Toulouse</term>, who said to him in these exact terms: "You despise the seven Sacraments of our Salvation—the Sacrament of the body and blood of Jesus Christ in the <term>Eucharist</term>.—You claim that the holy Cross, adored by the universal church as a sign of Salvation, is a diabolical and cursed sign, etc." Benoit Moliners also confessed in 1301 (2): "that <term>Transubstantiation</term> <note>The miracle where the bread and wine of the mass are believed to become the actual body and blood of Christ</note> in the Eucharist was an impossibility."

***

(1) One has only to consult the 219 propositions condemned in 1277 by the Bishop of Paris, Etienne II <note>Also known as Stephen Tempier; his "Condemnations of 1277" targeted various philosophical and theological ideas he deemed dangerous</note>. There are several among them that derive immediately from the dogmas of the <term>Gnostics</term>, and they sufficiently show the fermentation that then reigned in people's minds; for example, No. 1. That God is not three and one, since the Trinity does not consist with supreme simplicity. <note>original: "Quod Deus non est trinus & unus, quoniam Trinitas non stat cum summa simplicitate."</note> No. 2. That God cannot generate an equal to himself, for that which is generated by another has a beginning; <note>original: "Quod Deus non potest generare sibi similem, quod enim generatur ab aliquo, habet principium;"</note> and the bold proposition, No. 37, that nothing is to be believed unless it is self-evident, or can be explained from self-evident things. <note>original: "quod non est credendum nisi per se notum sit, vel ex per se notis possit declarari."</note> All these propositions were collected by Schneider, in his *Library of Ecclesiastical History*, Vol. I. Notebook. Weimar 1781. large 8vo.

(2) Fuessli at the place cited, Vol. I, p. 417.

that the relationship between signs and objects was founded in the essence of things; and upon this, a system of <term>theurgy</term> <note>A form of ritual magic intended to invoke the presence of or communicate with divine beings</note> was established—a reciprocal communication between the visible world and the invisible world. <header>ESSAY ON ACCUSATIONS</header>

Indeed, one cannot form an idea of the revolting excesses that the authors of <term>Ecclesiastical History</term> <note>Historical accounts of the Christian Church, often written by proponents of the dominant "orthodox" view</note>, and even the <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>The influential early Christian theologians and scholars whose writings established the foundations of Christian doctrine</note> themselves, have allowed themselves at all times against <term>Heretics</term>. My heart bled when I read the *heresies of Epiphanius* <note>Referring to the "Panarion" (Medicine Chest) written by Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 315–403), a work that describes and refutes eighty different heresies with often polemical and harsh language</note>. The fault is always on the side of the heretics; they are condemned in everything; even the most innocent things are turned to their disadvantage; in even the most serious accusations, there is never any question of listening to the opposing party; no one thinks to cite circumstances that might seem excusable; no one takes the trouble to examine whether the facts presented as ridiculous are susceptible to a reasonable explanation; whether a certain step judged impious does not stem from a principle of misunderstood devotion; whether in accusations that are entirely unbelievable there might not be...

> "...enemies. If the <term>Catholic</term> <note>In this context, referring to members of the established, "universal" orthodox Church as opposed to "heretical" sects</note> has thought the same as the Heretic, shall the latter be defamed as a magician, as a man worthy of the fire, while the former is justified or excused?" <note>original: "Si le Catholique a penſé comme l'Hérétique, le dernier ſera-t-il diffamé comme un magicien, comme un homme digne du feu, pendant que l'on juſtifiera, ou que l'on excuſera le premier ?"</note> *History of Manichaeism* <note>Isaac de Beausobre's "Histoire critique de Manichée et du Manichéisme" (1734), a seminal work that challenged traditional views on the Manichaean heresy</note>, Volume II, p. 48.

The author highlights the double standard where the same beliefs might be excused in an orthodox Catholic but condemned as magic or heresy in others.</summary>

# ON

# THE ORIGIN OF THE SOCIETY

# OF

# FREEMASONS.

<term>Freemasons</term> (original: *Franc-Maçons*): A fraternal organization that rose to prominence during the Enlightenment, known for its use of architectural symbols—like the compass and square—to teach moral lessons and its emphasis on rational inquiry and brotherhood.

<term>Society</term> (original: *Société*): In this historical context, a "society" refers to a private association or organized community of individuals bound by shared rules and secrets.

<term>Origin</term> (original: *Origine*): This refers to the historical and mythological roots of the group. During the 18th century, many writers debated whether Freemasonry descended from ancient Egyptian priests, medieval cathedral builders, or the Knights Templar.

<summary>This is the title page for a treatise investigating the historical and philosophical roots of the Freemasons. It establishes the subject matter as an inquiry into how and where this influential fraternal society began.</summary>

<term>decrees</term> (original: *décrets*): Official orders or edicts issued by a legal or religious authority. In this period, both the Catholic Church and various European monarchies issued decrees condemning Freemasonry.

<term>prohibitions</term> (original: *défenses*): Formal bans; the text suggests that by banning the society, authorities actually created more suspicion and false rumors than if they had allowed open discussion.

<term>erroneous opinions</term> (original: *opinions erronnées*): Misguided or false beliefs. The author argues that secrecy and censorship breed conspiracy theories and misunderstandings.

<term>spirit of inquiry</term> (original: *esprit de recherche*): A core value of the Enlightenment, emphasizing that human progress relies on the freedom to investigate, debate, and use reason rather than blindly following dogma.

# ON THE

## ORIGIN OF THE SOCIETY

### OF

# FREEMASONS.

<term>Freemasons</term> (original: *Franc-Maçons*): A fraternal organization that arose from the medieval guilds of stonemasons. By the 18th century, it had transformed into a "speculative" society, meaning members used the tools and language of building as symbols for architectural moral character and social progress.

<term>Society</term> (original: *Société*): In this context, it refers to a "secret society" or private association. During the Enlightenment, such societies were often viewed with suspicion by monarchs and the Church because they promoted equality among members and free discussion outside of state control.

<term>Origin</term> (original: *Origine*): The historical and often legendary roots of the order. Masonic texts from this era frequently attempted to trace their lineage back to the construction of Solomon’s Temple, the Crusades, or ancient mystery schools to establish legitimacy.

<note>This title page is set in a bold, decorative layout typical of 18th-century treatises. It introduces a historical investigation into where the Freemasons came from, likely moving from the philosophical defenses of the previous section into a more historical or chronological narrative.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a new section of the manuscript dedicated to investigating the historical and traditional origins of the Freemasons.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, origins, history, secret societies, title page</keywords>

# CONCERNING
## THE ORIGIN OF THE SOCIETY
### OF THE
# FREEMASONS.

---

Since the Knights Templar were united by manifold bonds, such as the institution of the Order in itself, political views, and their mysterious worship, it is not probable that their abolition suddenly caused every kind of interaction between them to cease. We see that the former Jesuits <note>The Society of Jesus was suppressed by Pope Clement XIV in 1773; the author uses this recent event to explain how members of a banned group remain loyal to one another.</note> still possess an <term>esprit de corps<gloss>a sense of shared identity and pride among members of a group</gloss></term>, which serves as a rallying point for them; it is inevitable that the same thing occurred among the Templars in an entirely similar situation, for a command or a prohibition is not sufficient to root out the inclinations or opinions of men at a moment's notice. It was therefore enough for the Templars, just like the Jesuits, to have the hope—however chimerical <note>unrealistic or imaginary</note>—of seeing some—

<meta>catchword: some-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the suppression of the Knights Templar did not immediately end their brotherhood, drawing a historical parallel with the resilience of the Jesuits after their own dissolution. The author suggests that shared beliefs and group loyalty naturally survive official legal bans.</summary>

day the re-establishment of their Order, so that they avoided for a long time a total dispersion which would have left them without hope. It is true that nothing proves these subsequent connections; and although we see in history that there was more than once a question, in those times and in different places, of the re-establishment of the Templars, nonetheless the connection of any Order of Chivalry currently existing with the one of which we speak is a thing so difficult to establish on a truly historical basis—and this point of history has always been handled with such clumsiness by the great number of writers—that in this, as in all things which depend on faith, it is appropriate to leave each person to their own belief.

He claims, in his *Continuation of the work of Ernst and Falk* <note>Referring to *Ernst and Falk: Dialogues for Freemasons* (1778), a famous philosophical work by Lessing exploring the purpose of the brotherhood.</note>, page 53 (1), that the Masonries of the Templars were in high repute during the twelfth and thirteenth centuries; and that it was from one of these Templar Masonries, still existing in London, that Christopher Wren <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723), the architect of St. Paul's Cathedral in London; a tradition exists that he was a Grand Master of Freemasons, though this is debated by modern historians.</note> had, toward the end of the last century, drawn both the idea and the substance of the Society of Freemasons of which he is the founder. ---

(1) This work, although printed without his consent, is indisputably his.

(2) It is very likely that he has in mind the different degrees <note>In this context, "degrees" refers to the progressive stages of initiation within a secret society or chivalric order.</note> of the Templars; however, one could not say they were in high repute, since it was the condemnation of the Order that revealed them; moreover, I find it unlikely that a secret community, formed from a remnant of the Templars, could have existed for four hundred years without any significant motive. If such a community was found in London in the seventeenth century, it may have a more ancient source, without absolutely tracing back to the beginning of the fourteenth century.

Six years ago, my late friend <note>The author refers to Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), the prominent Enlightenment philosopher and dramatist.</note>, stopping in Berlin on his return from Italy, spoke to me in great detail about his hypothesis regarding the origin of the Freemasons; he attributed it to Christopher Wren <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723), the architect of St. Paul's Cathedral. 18th-century Masonic tradition often claimed him as a Grand Master, a claim scrutinized by later historians.</note>, at the time of the construction of St. Paul's Cathedral in London, and he added that the seed of this association had existed for centuries, though he did not speak then of that secret Masonry of the Templars, which Christopher Wren was said to have merely modified to establish his own (1). ...will not be long before it is made public; in the meantime, I will share with the public the result of my reflections on this matter.

What does the word *Maçonnerie*—in English, *Masonry*—mean? Lessing <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), a famous German philosopher and author of "Ernst and Falk," a series of dialogues on Freemasonry.</note> says on page 47 that *Masa* signifies, in Anglo-Saxon, a "table," and *Masonry*, consequently, a "private table society." I do not know if *Masa* means a table, but I do know that in Anglo-Saxon *Maça* means "companion" (1); and I have not found that in the writings of the Middle Ages, *Masonia* ever signified a "table society." Scholars very well-versed in this branch of literature have been no more successful than I; it may be that here again the papers of the deceased will give us the necessary clarifications, although it must be admitted that the passage from Agricola (2) to which he alludes and which I have found, is nothing less than a—

---

> (1) *Maça*: an equal, an associate, a partner, a spouse, a peer, an equal, a companion, a mate. <note>Original Latin definitions provided: "par, socius, consors, conjux."</note> See Somner’s *Saxon-Latin-English Dictionary* <note>William Somner (1598–1669), an English antiquarian who published the "Dictionarium Saxonico-Latino-Anglicum" in 1689.</note>, in folio, Oxford 1689. Ihre <note>Johan Ihre (1707–1780), a Swedish philologist.</note>, in his *Glossary*, derives the well-known word *Mat-opi* <note>A term related to "mess-mates" or "table companions."</note> from *Mate*.
>
> (2) The assembly of the Knights of the Round Table was called *Messeney* in German. See Joseph Agricola <note>Likely referring to a historical or genealogical work by a writer of this name.</note>, Folio 323.

<meta>This page continues the author’s etymological investigation into the origins of the word "Masonry," specifically challenging Lessing’s theory that it derives from a word for "table." # OF THE FREEMASONS.

...ancient source; furthermore, *Messeney* <note>A term used by the 15th-century writer Rudolf Agricola to describe the Knights of the Round Table.</note>, as Agricola writes it, is not yet *Massoney*. I believe I have found an entirely different origin for this term. *Massonya* in medieval Latin is the same thing as *Clava*, a mace <note>A heavy wooden staff or club used as a weapon.</note> (1); but *Clava* is also used for *Clavis*, a key, and from there comes the word *Clavare* (2), which denotes the right to enter a house and to refuse entry to another.

But we see that what we call in German a closed, private, exclusive society—as if one were to say a <term>societas clavata</term> <note>Latin: "a society under key" or "locked society."</note>—is expressed in English by the same word for a mace, "*Club*." Does this not lead back to our *Massonya* (3), which has the same connections?

---

> (1) Carpentier, *Glossary of Medieval Latin*, Vol. II, *Massonya*. <note>Pierre Carpentier (1697–1767) was a scholar who updated the definitive dictionary of medieval Latin terms.</note>
>
> (2) The same, Vol. I, *Clava*, *Clavare*. Likewise <note>original Latin: "Item"</note>: "that the Lord Abbot—may bolt and close the houses of the said men." <note>original: "quod Dom. Abbas — possit clavare & claudere domus dictorum hominum."</note>
>
> (3) If one does not wish to adopt such a natural origin, I do not know from where one would derive the word *Club*, insofar as it signifies a private society; for the etymology that Skinner <note>Stephen Skinner (1623–1667), an English physician and linguist.</note> and after him Johnson <note>Samuel Johnson (1709–1784), author of the famous 1755 English dictionary.</note> provide for it is enormously forced, deriving it from the Anglo-Saxon *Cleoban*, to split, because the "shot" <note>The bill or total expense at a tavern or meeting.</note> was divided and shared among all the members.

<summary>The author proposes that "Masonry" and "Club" share a linguistic root in the medieval Latin word for mace and key, suggesting the society is defined by its exclusivity and the power to "lock" its doors. Could Lessing <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), a German philosopher and Freemason whose dialogues "Ernst and Falk" investigated the purpose of the order.</note> perhaps have had reason to believe that his worthy friend, Leu de Filneck <note>A character in Lessing's famous play *Nathan the Wise* who is revealed to be a Knight Templar. Furthermore, whether the Freemasons <term>Franc-maçons</term> <note>The French term for the fraternal organization.</note> derive their origin from that particular masonry or not is something I am not examining at present. To prove a fact of such importance requires incontestable documents rather than suppositions and traditions; however, since I am on this subject, I will report here what is known to me regarding the beginnings of this society. Yet, I can only communicate to the public a few results of my painstaking research; even if prudence permitted me to offer my entire work, I would fear falling into tedious lengths that the reader's patience could not endure. <meta>This page continues the author's discussion on the methodology of his research. The previous page ended with the catchword "In the," leading into the opening sentence regarding the difficulty of sifting through historical records.</meta>

->### ORIGIN OF THE SOCIETY<-

H

research of this nature, one must find the few grains of truth contained within an unbearable jumble of old and bad books; there is enough there to bore many a reader, without much hope of interesting a similar number. If I cannot establish everything I claim through proofs drawn from history, at least I shall say nothing that history contradicts.

If I treat the origin of this famous association, it is in the hope that no one will interpret my motives unfavorably. I seek neither to describe its establishments nor to penetrate its secrets; that is not the object of my work, for one cannot count among its secrets any of its external symbols, since they can be found in works approved by the majority of its members.

My goal in writing this allows me to disregard entirely the "secret" of a society which I view as a phenomenon in the history of the human race. A work titled *The Blazing Star* <note>original: "l’Etoile flamboyante." This refers to a seminal Masonic text published in 1766 by Baron de Tschoudy, which explored the hermetic and philosophical roots of the order.</note> puts the number of Freemasons <term>Franc-maçons</term> <note>The French term for members of the fraternal order of Freemasonry.</note> at ten million.

If this famous secret does exist, there is no less reason to be surprised that such a numerous society could maintain such unity and, above all, such marvelous discretion—two things of which the uninitiated <term>profanes</term> <note>From the Latin "profanus," meaning "outside the temple." N.* <note>The "N. N." likely refers to the *Noachites* or perhaps the *Illuminati* (often abbreviated or masked in contemporary polemics), or it may refer to an anonymous work.</note>—enriching history with his own ideas (1); gathering all mysterious societies into one, however different they may have been; and imagining those that never existed;

(1) This writer speaks, as one might expect, of the Templars, and informs us among other things (p. 111) that at their reception <note>Referring to the initiation ceremony of the Knights Templar.</note> they placed their foot upon the cross and upon the triangle, and that they worshipped a three-headed figure surrounded by circles and death's heads. Nothing here is fabricated except for the three heads, the circles, and the skulls. This author plays the scholar and bristles his book with a multitude of citations that are, unfortunately, useless, since they are not taken from original sources; on page 30, he further cites Chifflet’s dissertation, *On Basilidian Gems* <note>original: "de Gemmis Basilidianis." This 17th-century work by Jean-Jacques Chifflet studied ancient engraved stones associated with the Gnostic teacher Basilides.</note>; but he has read it no better than the other works he quotes, for if he had only glanced at the plates <note>The engraved illustrations in the book.</note>, would he have neglected the two important stones that I have had engraved on the frontispiece of this treatise?

When one wishes to write a true History, one must assert nothing as certain except that which can be proven by incontestable documents, drawn from original sources and contemporary authors; even then, one must exercise caution. One must consider that similar things are not the same, and that *after this* is not always *because of this.* <note>The author is referring to the logical fallacy *post hoc ergo propter hoc*, which assumes that because one event follows another, the first must be the cause of the second.</note> Tradition is fine for those who are already convinced, or who wish to be, and I leave them to their convictions with all my heart. Suppositions or hypotheses will never take the place of proof; it is not that they lack merit, but for them to be accepted, they must have a very direct connection to established facts, and the concurrence of all circumstances must give them the highest [degree of probability...]

...degree of probability. <note>This sentence completes a thought from the previous page regarding the standards of historical evidence.</note> But to forcibly join together facts that have nothing in common, to skip over entire eras, and to disregard the most manifest contradictions with generally accepted notions—all for the sake of establishing a favorite **system**—is no longer writing **history**; it is dreaming. Even great talents combined with extensive knowledge cannot prevent it from being anything other than a dream.

I do not see how a claim to great **antiquity** would make the society of **Freemasons** more illustrious. It is in the current <term>constitution<note>In this 18th-century context, "constitution" refers to the internal laws, moral structure, and organized form of the society as it exists in the author's present day.</note></term> of a body, and not in its origin, that one must seek its utility. ...was then a young man full of fire, [who] saw the defects of the sciences, theology, and morals of his time; that he sought to purge them, and that to achieve this he had imagined uniting into a single body all those who, like him, were zealous admirers of the morally good and beautiful. This exhortation is accompanied by the allegorical tale of the discovery of the tomb of Father Rose-Cross (1), an allegory under the veil of which the designs and beneficial effects of the projected society are presented. *The Chemical Wedding* is attributed to Father Rose-Cross <note>Referring to the legendary founder Christian Rosenkreuz.</note>, who is said to have written it in 1459; but one recognizes in it the tone of the beginning of the seventeenth century and especially the style of J. ---

(1) This name "Rose-Cross" is itself allegorical. The cross represents the holiness of the union, and the rose is the image of discretion. These two words joined together signify a "holy discretion": the rose was always its symbol, as witnessed by the ancient proverb *under the rose* <note>original: *sub rosa*; a Latin phrase referring to matters told in confidence.</note>; from this come the three roses on the apron of the Freemasons and those which they distribute to one another.

---

(1) Even today, every mason <note>Meaning a practical, working stonemason</note> in England and Scotland enjoys a right of preference for being received as a Freemason, and he only pays half of the reception fee; which proves that it is regarded here as reciprocal.

(2) Ashmole was an antiquarian in the manner of his time, when things were collected without choice or taste and everything ancient was respected. He therefore searched through English Antiquities for everything that might concern the masons, and since the Freemasons formed a single body with them, he extended to the newcomers what only concerned the former. It is remarkable that Ashmole, one of the first members of the society, had already contested the tradition that traced their descent from a troop of Italian Builders <note>original Latin: *Camentariorum Societas*</note>, and others, in whose favor the Pope had granted a Bull during the reign of Henry III. He said that these were actual artisan-masons. See his life in the *Biographia Britannica*.

They had a common goal, and the difference in their methods arose only from the difference found in some of their opinions; one held as a maxim that the knowledge of nature should be spread throughout all orders of society, while according to the other, the very nature of this science required that its secrets be the portion of a chosen few: it is based on this principle that the latter <note>Referring to the group that became the Freemasons</note> shrouded both its meetings and its transactions in mystery; although, however, one cannot prove that from that time it made a show of its secret, it had a mystery—in English, *mystery*. felt their hope reborn, they understood that such critical times required a very particular circumspection <note>A cautious and watchful approach to one's circumstances</note>; add to that that a member of their society, Sir Richard Wallis (1), became suspect, to the point that he lost all their confidence. Their motto was "let wisdom rest upon you" (2). They changed their sign <note>The secret gestures or handshakes used for identification</note> again, and in their interviews they warned each other allegorically to take care, in this shaky state, of falling, so as not to break one's arm.

There is, therefore, nothing true in all that the anonymous author asserts, except that this revolution was supported in secret by a certain society; but it was by that of the Freemasons, which—except for the period of its founding—had nothing in common with the other <note>Meaning the scientific group that became the Royal Society.</note> and which, in literature as in politics, even held entirely opposite principles.

It continued to meet after the great era of 1660 and even made several regulations in 1663 aimed at its preservation (2); but its zeal was naturally destined to decrease, as a consequence of the considerable changes that customs and sciences underwent under the reign of Charles I <note>The text specifies Charles I, though the historical context of the 1660 Restoration and the 1663 regulations suggests the author is discussing the era of Charles II or the transition following the era of Charles I.</note>.

### ORIGIN OF THE SOCIETY

<meta>catchword: for</meta>

Its political activities ceased with the King's accession to the Crown, and as for its primary objective, which was to cultivate the sciences esoterically <term>esoterically: knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge.</term>, great gaps must have formed in its system between 1646 and 1680. The **Royal Society**, which followed a completely opposite plan, had made great efforts since 1660—followed by great success—to eliminate the distinction in philosophy between the two methods: the exoteric <term>exoteric: knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by the general public.</term> and the esoteric. Several **Freemasons** who were the most staunchly in favor of the latter had died; others had followed the progress of their century in their opinions. The famous **Elias Ashmole** <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692) was a celebrated English antiquary, politician, and student of alchemy, often cited as one of the first recorded "speculative" Freemasons.</note> soon left them, and, as his historian puts it, "took a civil leave of his comrades." He had nonetheless been a great partisan of the esoteric method, and after having produced significant works on alchemy (1), after having

It is not part of my plan to examine what the secret and private reasons for these changes might have been; but I ask the reader to recall here the violent unrest caused in England by King James II’s <term>James II: The last Catholic monarch of England, Scotland, and Ireland, whose reign (1685–1688) was marked by struggles over religious liberty and the limits of monarchical power, ending in his deposition during the Glorious Revolution.</term> inclination toward Despotism and Papism <note>original: "Papiſme". > (1) I mention here in passing the **Society of the Trowel** (*Compagnia della cazzuola* <note>original: "casuola"</note>), which a joke gave rise to in Florence in 1512, and which later included among its members people of renown, scholars, and artists. Its emblem was the trowel and the hammer, and Saint Andrew was its patron. They held several festivals, in one of which all the members appeared in masons' clothing. This Society had no other purpose than pleasure, just like the **Society of the Cauldron** (*Compagnia del paiuolo* <note>original: "sajuolo"</note>), which existed in Florence at the same time. This Society and that of our Freemasons have absolutely nothing in common. See Vasari, *Lives of the Painters* <note>original: "Vite di Pittori"</note>, Rome 1760, in the *Life of the Sculptor G. F. Rustici*.

# ON FREEMASONS.

...a problem that I leave to those who can boast of knowing equally well both what the Freemasons do and what is advantageous to humankind. At this moment, a fable comes to mind that I read somewhere, and the relevance of the topic invites me to include it here.

A man, having found an excellent fabric, made a large cloak from it that perfectly suited his purpose—which was to blend into the crowd and go his way *incognito* <note>original: "incognito". Meaning in disguise or without being recognized.</note>, well-covered and well-protected against bad weather. This man was recognized as a wise man; consequently, all the fools set about imitating him. But how did they go about it? They copied the cut and the color of the garment without paying any attention to the fabric; and, although the rain and wind penetrated everywhere, they were hardly bothered by it because, unlike the inventor who had made the cloak to be sheltered, they had made it so that they would be noticed. If one of these gentlemen shivered with cold, his vanity consoled him whenever he heard a common man cry out: "See,

**TAUTE:** <note>Referring to Reinhold Taute, a famous Masonic bibliographer</note> Nicolai asserts that German Rosicrucianism <note>original: "Rosenkreuzerei"</note> arrived in England via <unclear>XXXXX</unclear> FLUDD <note>Robert Fludd, a famous English physician and mystical philosopher</note> in the 1620s <note>original: "3. Dezennium d. 17. Jhs."</note>, where it was cultivated and later flourished as Freemasonry. Regarding the Knights Templar, Nicolai is dependent on the work of DUPUY <note>Pierre Dupuy, a 17th-century historian known for his work on the trial of the Templars</note>.

<vocab>Templars, Freemasonry, Nicolai, Beyerle, Rosicrucianism, Fludd, Dupuy, Freemasonry</vocab>

<summary>This bibliographic card describes a rare 17th-century French translation of Friedrich Nicolai's work on the Knights Templar and their alleged secret links to the origins of Freemasonry. The notes highlight Nicolai's theory that Rosicrucianism was a precursor to Freemasonry in England.</summary>
<keywords>Knights Templar, Freemasonry, Rosicrucianism, Friedrich Nicolai, Robert Fludd, bibliography, rare books, secret societies</keywords>
